Bill Ackman on the worst position in his portfolio and why he is still holding it

Yahoo Finance(1.5M)·17:51·2026-11-13
Bill AckmanFounder and CEO, Pershing Square Capital Management Brian SozziExecutive Editor, Yahoo Finance
TL;DRBill Ackman identifies Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac as his portfolio's worst-performing yet highest-upside position, while explaining why dominant cash-generating compounders like Microsoft and Netflix remain core holdings despite recent market turbulence.
Return to Market Sanity: Shift Toward Fundamentals: Bill Ackman notes markets are pivoting back toward cash flows and business quality rather than short-term momentum. Shiny Objects vs. Quality: Disruption-focused AI plays and "Neo Clouds" have been treated as speculative...

TL;DR

Bill Ackman identifies Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac as his portfolio's worst-performing yet highest-upside position, while explaining why dominant cash-generating compounders like Microsoft and Netflix remain core holdings despite recent market turbulence.

Market Sanity & Investment Philosophy

  • Return to Market Sanity: Shift Toward Fundamentals: Bill Ackman notes markets are pivoting back toward cash flows and business quality rather than short-term momentum.
      • Shiny Objects vs. Quality: Disruption-focused AI plays and "Neo Clouds" have been treated as speculative shiny objects, leading to discounted valuations for high-quality, dominant compounders.
      • Deployment Pace: Pershing Square has made seven new investments in the past six months, taking advantage of compressed valuations on long-desired companies.
  • Macro & Valuation Outlook: Balanced S&P 500: Ackman views the overall stock market as reasonably valued with strong earnings growth, declining to make short-term predictions on the Nasdaq.
      • Compute as an Asset: He is a believer in owning compute over time, provided it is held in a non-leveraged format and tied to defensible business models.

Fannie Mae, Freddie Mac, and High-Upside Special Situations

  • Contrarian High-Conviction Bet: Worst Position with Highest Upside: Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac represent Pershing Square's worst-performing position down over 60% this calendar year (costing 800-900 basis points of performance), yet Ackman views them as $4 stocks with a true value of $40 to $50.
      • 13-Year Holding Period: Pershing Square has held the stocks for 13 years betting on release from conservatorship and privatization.
      • Political Catalysts: The thesis rests on presidential action—noting prior positive movement during Trump's term, a commitment letter from 2021, and recent comments by Howard Lutnick indicating potential movement.
      • Asymmetric Risk-Reward: Midterm political shifts handicap broad economic policy but leave Fannie and Freddie as a prime executive-action opportunity. Treasury Secretary comments suggest addressing the companies in the back half of the term.

Big Tech, AI Infrastructure, and Software Defensibility

  • Microsoft Enterprise Moat: Enterprise Stickiness: Microsoft remains a strong core holding due to its embedded position across 450 million business seats, robust cybersecurity, and centralized control over corporate AI usage.
      • Hyper scaler Gateways: Anthropic routes nearly 50% of its revenues through hyperscalers like Amazon and Microsoft, proving that distribution channels remain vital.
  • Netflix Valuation & Prospects: Compelling Valuation: Netflix trades at 16x to 17x earnings (down from mid-30s), representing a significant discount from its highs despite strong subscription growth and renewals.
      • Growth Drivers: Catalysts for next year include passage of time, continued earnings and revenue growth, content wins, and improved subscriber disclosure.
  • NVIDIA & Jensen Huang: Character Defense: Bill Ackman publicly defended NVIDIA CEO Jensen Huang, citing personal experience with Huang's help on a medical project for Ackman's daughter and advocating an "innocent until proven guilty" stance.
      • Founder Succession: While acknowledging Huang is an irreplaceable founder-led visionary akin to Steve Jobs, Ackman believes NVIDIA remains well-positioned for the long term.

Why Anthropic Does Not Fit the Portfolio

  • High-Capital Risk Profile: Complex Financial Modeling: While Ackman calls Anthropic perhaps the most amazing business story of the century, its requirement for massive capital investments makes valuation and future cash flows difficult to nail down with precision.
      • Speculative Bet: Unlike a widget company or Visa, companies requiring continuous massive capital outlays carry a higher risk if revenue acceleration misses projections or capital access tightens.
      • Operating Profitability: While Anthropic approaches operating profitability, its long-term business model depends heavily on maintaining a leading position in compute and AI capabilities.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Bill Ackman predicts that if the president follows through on previous commitments regarding Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac, they will become $40 to $50 stocks from their current $4 level.
  • Bill Ackman predicts the Treasury will address the conservatorship of Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac in the back half of the presidential term rather than prior to the midterms.

Notable Quotes

"The worst-performing position is also the one I think has the most upside: Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac, which our firm has held for 13 years on a bet they are released from conservatorship." — Bill Ackman
"Anytime you have a business that requires a massive investment in order to continue to accelerate its growth... it's a higher risk proposition. We're kind of we think of ourselves as very low-risk investors." — Bill Ackman
"If you're the gateway to how people interacts with the basic workflows of a desk worker... which is what Microsoft is today, I think it's a much more difficult position to dislodge." — Bill Ackman

Cyberattacks Used to Take Months. AI Can Do It in Minutes.

a16z(309.0K)·46:07·6.0K views·2026-11-13
Ami LuttwakCo-founder and CTO, Wiz Zane LackeyGeneral Partner, a16z Sarah WangGeneral Partner, a16z
TL;DRAI is collapsing the timeline for cyberattacks from months to just 20 minutes, marking the beginning of "Black October"—a wave of unprecedented, agentic attacks where AI-driven offensive capabilities are outperforming human hackers.
The Definition of Black October: Ami Luttwak characterizes October as a black swan event and a turning point for cybersecurity, citing an unprecedented volume and severity of vulnerabilities combined with new AI-powered speed and expertise. Collapsed Exploit Timelines: AI has...

TL;DR

AI is collapsing the timeline for cyberattacks from months to just 20 minutes, marking the beginning of "Black October"—a wave of unprecedented, agentic attacks where AI-driven offensive capabilities are outperforming human hackers.

The AI Offensive Surge and "Black October"

  • The Definition of Black October: Ami Luttwak characterizes October as a black swan event and a turning point for cybersecurity, citing an unprecedented volume and severity of vulnerabilities combined with new AI-powered speed and expertise.
  • Collapsed Exploit Timelines: AI has compressed the time required to write complex exploits from weeks or months down to roughly 20 minutes.
  • The Open-Source Threat Multiplier: While proprietary frontier models advanced vulnerability discovery months ago, open-source models have now caught up, placing sophisticated offensive capabilities into a much wider set of hands.
  • Real-World Impact on Startups:
    • AI development platforms are being specifically targeted because they hold valuable AI credentials and API keys.
    • Startups are a prime target for LLM-driven attacks, facing threats that can steal keys and drain accounts within minutes.
  • Case Study: PHP2Shell:
    • Affecting a technology running on roughly 70% of the internet, PHP2Shell impacted nearly 100% of Wiz's customer base.
    • While 50% of customers patched within the first 2 to 3 days, complete industry-wide resolution will take years due to unpatchable older versions and delayed Linux distribution updates.
  • AI vs. Human Capability: Ami Luttwak notes that cybersecurity is almost the only domain where AI is now demonstrably better than humans, executing complex zero-day attacks (such as on kernel drivers) at a frequency never before possible.

Defensive Strategies: Pipelines, Automation, and Red Agents

  • The New Engineering Challenge: Developing secure AI systems requires managing costs via intelligent, multi-tiered pipelines rather than running expensive frontier models on every single event.
    • Blue Agent Pipeline: Starts with deterministic detection, moves through cheap model detection, escalates to advanced models, and finally uses the most advanced model to confirm maliciousness before alerting the customer.
    • Red Agent Pipeline: Begins with deterministic exposure analysis, followed by an exposure agent looking at unauthenticated internet-facing endpoints, and scales up to deep pentests and code analysis for critical applications.
  • Continuous Testing vs. Static Scanning: Static scans (like early Mitos scans) can waste millions of dollars without providing continuous protection. Security must shift to a continuous, intelligent model that balances internal code analysis, disk scans, and runtime monitoring.
  • Prioritizing External Exposure: Ami Luttwak emphasizes that scanning unauthenticated external attack surfaces (such as hardcoded keys in mobile apps) is far more urgent and effective than immediately diving into code scanning.
  • Automated Remediation Constraints: While new startups can build automated CI/CD and agentic dev-to-patch pipelines from day one, legacy companies face massive hurdles due to obscure organizational knowledge and legacy codebases.

Security Budgets, Architecture, and Industry Transformation

  • Security vs. Compliance Work: Ami Luttwak argues that most of what traditional security teams do is not actual security; true security focuses on proactive preparation (red teaming and guardrails), critical risk mitigation, and response readiness.
  • The Limitations of Budgets: Increasing security budgets and buying traditional tools (like classic CSPM configuration check lists) will not solve the modern crisis. The real solution requires fundamental secure architecture—sandboxing, isolation, network lockdown, and process lockdown.
  • Obsolescence of Legacy Stack: CISOs are using the current crisis to reboot their security vendor stacks, phasing out legacy tools in favor of AI-native technologies built for modern cloud and AI threats.
  • Public Infrastructure Risks: Beyond tech enterprises, real-world legacy infrastructure—such as refrigeration units and public services—is connected to the internet and acutely vulnerable to mass-scale disruption. In response, Wiz launched Scan for Good to help public services and nonprofits identify exposures.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Ami Luttwak predicts that the only companies able to survive the next several years will be those that actively build security automation, conduct continuous security reviews, and redesign their internet-exposed systems for zero-trust environments.
  • Zane Lackey predicts that the industry is returning to an era reminiscent of the 1990s and 2000s, characterized by persistent unauthenticated zero-days, frequent breaches, and the potential resurgence of internet worms similar to the Windows XP days.

Notable Quotes

"We are calling this Black October... since it's a black swan event. We've never seen, never ever seen so many security incidents happen in a single month." — Ami Luttwak
"Most of what security teams do is not security... security in my view is getting either ready for the attacker attacking yourself or putting guardrails for the attack when it comes." — Ami Luttwak
"We are at an era where AI is better than humans. I think the only companies that will be able to survive are companies that are building now not just security automation, security design, security reviews." — Ami Luttwak

Ep. 044 - Is NVIDIA Over? EMERGENCY Pod with ChinaTalk | Dylan Patel, Jordan Nanos, Jordan Schneider

SemiAnalysis(23.6K)·51:27·2026-11-13
Jordan NanosHost, SemiAnalysis Dylan PatelAnalyst, SemiAnalysis Jordan SchneiderHost, ChinaTalk
TL;DRFollowing Jensen Huang’s sudden resignation as CEO of NVIDIA amid conduct violations and chip-smuggling allegations, Dylan Patel, Jordan Nanos, and Jordan Schneider debate the future of US-China export controls and NVIDIA’s ability to execute.
While the panelists agree that the US should prioritize equipment bans (the MATCH Act) over cloud-rental bans (RASA), they diverge on whether NVIDIA will suffer severe execution slips without its founder or remain dominant over the next several years. Conduct Violations vs....

TL;DR

Following Jensen Huang’s sudden resignation as CEO of NVIDIA amid conduct violations and chip-smuggling allegations, Dylan Patel, Jordan Nanos, and Jordan Schneider debate the future of US-China export controls and NVIDIA’s ability to execute. While the panelists agree that the US should prioritize equipment bans (the MATCH Act) over cloud-rental bans (RASA), they diverge on whether NVIDIA will suffer severe execution slips without its founder or remain dominant over the next several years.

Jensen Huang’s Exit & The Smuggling Evidence

  • Conduct Violations vs. Export Control Complicity: Jordan Nanos notes that while Huang’s exit was triggered by conduct violations (affairs with subordinates), the underlying issue is circumstantial evidence of chip smuggling to China.
      • Nanos points to the indictment of NVIDIA’s Taiwan sales manager, the arrest of Supermicro COO and co-founder Wally, and a Supermicro sales manager currently a fugitive in Taiwan.
      • He highlights a September 2025 audit photo showing a Supermicro Taiwan employee using a hairdryer to move stickers between real and fake servers, noting that the flipped individual had deleted his encrypted messaging app upon arrest.
  • Intelligence Community Involvement: Nanos states the investigation appears to originate from the intelligence community, noting that Marco Rubio was briefed on related matters as early as May 2025.
  • Loss of Political Cover: Dylan Patel argues the NVIDIA board likely possesses evidence that Huang knew chips were reaching China, pointing to NVIDIA’s decision to stop reporting Singapore revenue. Patel adds that with the midterms ("blue wave") removing political cover for both Huang and Howard Lutnick, NVIDIA’s biggest defender against export restrictions is now off the table.
  • Willful Ignorance & Congressional Scrutiny: Jordan Schneider emphasizes that the issue is "willful ignorance" regarding Supermicro and Mega Speed. He warns that interim CEO and CFO Colette Kress will likely face days of congressional testimony, making an external, technical CEO preferable.

US-China Export Controls: MATCH Act vs. RASA

  • MATCH Act Over RASA: All three speakers express support for the MATCH Act (which blocks advanced DUV lithography tools, alongside EUV, from entering China) to force China to build lithography from scratch.
  • The Case Against RASA: Dylan Patel calls the RASA Act (which closes the cloud-access loophole used by Alibaba, Tencent, and ByteDance via cutouts in Malaysia) "dumb."
      • Retaliation Risk: Patel warns a total compute ban will trigger full economic warfare, noting that China’s components are in every NVIDIA, Google, and Amazon server (including NVIDIA’s largest PCB supplier) and that China has previously threatened rare earths and US auto plants.
      • Margin Stacking & Oversight: Patel argues that allowing Chinese firms to rent US GPUs abroad gives the US intelligence oversight, a kill switch, and massive profits—$7 million per megawatt for NVIDIA and $7–10 million per megawatt for cloud providers—while giving US firms like Meta a 4x compute cost advantage over ByteDance.
  • Pushback on Retaliation: Jordan Schneider pushes back on Patel’s fear of escalation, arguing China’s economy is stagnant with 20% unemployment and noting Beijing did not retaliate against chip controls in 2022, 2023, or 2024. Schneider frames the goal as *relative capability*—widening the US-China delta even if the US ecosystem takes a hit.
  • Diffusion Framework Enforcement: Patel and Schneider note that the Biden-era diffusion framework (issued as BIS guidance in May 2025) was never formally repealed, only left unenforced via a footnote by Kessler.
  • Collateral Damage to AI Progress: Jordan Nanos warns that escalating tensions will sever mutually beneficial relationships with Chinese researchers at US frontier labs and engineers in China who help bring up new NVIDIA and AMD GPUs.

China’s Domestic Chips & Open-Source Models

  • Accelerating Domestic Adoption: Jordan Nanos notes China is already moving off NVIDIA GPUs regardless of US cloud laws.
      • In February, GLM was released with support for seven domestic accelerators: Huawei, Moore Threads, Hygon, Cambricon, Kalray, MetaX, and Flame.
      • DeepSeek published a mega kernel with Huawei Ascend for its V4 model, and Meituan claims it trained LongCat entirely on Ascend.
  • Narrowing Hardware Gap: Nanos observes that while the US maintains a clear lead in frontier model capabilities, the chip capability gap is as close as it has ever been—Huawei’s Ascend chips are comparable to NVIDIA’s Hopper, and Huawei’s Kirin phone SoCs match or beat Apple’s. Schneider adds that China’s primary remaining hurdle is manufacturing scale beyond the 14nm line.
  • Open-Source Economics & Talent: Dylan Patel argues that Chinese labs like Moonshot and DeepSeek possess talent equal to top US labs, held back only by compute and deployment deficits. He notes that DeepMind, Grok, and Meta have regularly fallen behind Chinese open-source models despite higher spending, and that US application-layer companies running on open models (like Base 10 and Fireworks) generate well north of $10 billion in ARR. Nanos adds that NVIDIA’s own NeMoTron failed to beat Chinese open-source models despite heavy investment.

NVIDIA Without Jensen: Execution & Succession

  • Short-Term Stability vs. Long-Term Risk: Jordan Nanos argues NVIDIA is fine in the short term—it only needs to execute on the roadmap Huang set up for Rubin and Rubin Ultra, backed by its massive balance sheet and supply chain dominance. However, he warns of a potential internal "civil war" between finance (now in charge via Kress), technology, and sales, and notes that VCs will aggressively try to poach NVIDIA’s EVPs and SVPs (historically, almost no executives have left except Renee Haas to Arm).
  • Loss of Founder Intensity: Dylan Patel is more bearish, arguing Huang exerted even more granular control over NVIDIA than Steve Jobs did at Apple (e.g., overriding engineers 3 months before Volta tape-out to add Tensor Cores despite a 6-month estimate). Without Huang, Patel expects slower problem-solving, worse yield ramps, delayed D-specs on Rubin Ultra and Feynman, and a potential drop from NVIDIA’s ~70% supply chain allocation.
  • Apple Parallel: Jordan Schneider compares the moment to Apple’s 2015 transition from Jobs to Tim Cook, suggesting that even if NVIDIA executives leave to form new startups, it could benefit the broader Western AI ecosystem.
  • Succession & Nationalization: For the next permanent CEO, Patel favors Charlie Kawas (or Lisa Su / Johnny Srouji), while Nanos favors Hock Tan. Nanos briefly floats a theory that the intelligence community’s removal of Huang resembles the "nationalization" of NVIDIA (comparing it to Jack Ma), which Patel and Schneider flatly reject as the normal consequence of breaking export laws.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • NVIDIA’s 2029 Market Position: "It could be like in 2029 the best chips aren't Nvidia's and they don't have the majority of the supply chain." — *Dylan Patel, conditioning on execution slips and supply chain allocation losses without Huang.*
  • NVIDIA’s Medium-Term Survival: "I think there's a very, very high chance that they're still doing just fine two, three, four years from now." — *Jordan Nanos*
  • One-Year Talent Retention Test: "If a year from now we've seen like a bunch of departures from Nvidia, I'm going to be really concerned about their future." — *Jordan Nanos*
  • Internal Conflict: "There's potential that Nvidia just goes into civil war here over this cuz she's interim, she's not full-time... Finance is in charge now." — *Jordan Nanos*

Notable Quotes

"If your goal is to maintain a lead as large as possible for as long as possible and maintain control over China, you do not prevent China from having any compute because that actually means that the US will have no compute too." — *Dylan Patel*
"Hardware progresses slower without Jensen at the helm of Nvidia, period... This is a real slowdown in progress." — *Dylan Patel*
"In so far as anybody is pro AI, this is a bad day for AI progress. As tensions escalate, it seems like a bad day for AI progress." — *Jordan Nanos*
"The load star from a national interest perspective is relative capability... You need to have megawatts upon megawatts of advanced hardware in order to be able to both train and deliver this AI." — *Jordan Schneider*

FedEx CEO Raj Subramaniam & Alphabet Pres. Ruth Porat at Silicon Valley & The World | Semafor

Semafor(37.4K)·19:19·153 views·2026-11-13
Raj SubramaniamCEO, FedEx Ruth PoratPresident, Alphabet Liz HoffmanBusiness & Finance Editor, Semafor
TL;DRAlphabet President Ruth Porat and FedEx CEO Raj Subramaniam argued that massive AI infrastructure investments are justified by measurable economic returns—from boosting national GDP and small-business revenues to orchestrating $1.9 trillion in global supply chain inefficiencies—provided tech and logistics leaders secure local community buy-in and build on proprietary data foundations.
Alphabet’s "Done Right" Framework: Ruth Porat acknowledged that voter skepticism toward AI stems from both storytelling and execution failures around affordability and opportunity, prompting Alphabet to publish its "done right" community commitment. Energy & Resource...

TL;DR

Alphabet President Ruth Porat and FedEx CEO Raj Subramaniam argued that massive AI infrastructure investments are justified by measurable economic returns—from boosting national GDP and small-business revenues to orchestrating $1.9 trillion in global supply chain inefficiencies—provided tech and logistics leaders secure local community buy-in and build on proprietary data foundations.

Community Buy-In, Financing, and the Economics of AI Infrastructure

  • Alphabet’s "Done Right" Framework: Ruth Porat acknowledged that voter skepticism toward AI stems from both storytelling and execution failures around affordability and opportunity, prompting Alphabet to publish its "done right" community commitment.
    • Energy & Resource Protection: Alphabet commits to upfront transparency (no NDAs), investing in energy capacity and infrastructure even if unused so utilities can return value to ratepayers, and protecting local water and air.
    • Local Job Creation: Porat cited a visit to West Memphis, Arkansas (population 23,000) with Governor Sarah Huckabee Sanders and the mayor, noting that responsible data center commitments flip local polling from majority opposition to majority support.
  • Fortress Balance Sheet & Equity Issuance: Addressing why Alphabet, Meta, and Oracle have recently issued equity to fund AI buildouts, Porat stated that demand is eclipsing capacity because partners see real operating leverage and revenue growth.
    • Nubank Case Study: Porat highlighted Brazilian startup Nubank, which targeted the 40% unbanked population, drove down lending costs across incumbents, and is credited with boosting Brazil’s GDP by ~50 basis points (on ~2–2.5% overall growth).
    • Small Business Uplift: More than 80% of small businesses using AI report an uplift in revenue, earnings, and hiring.
    • Unit Economics: Porat noted that agentic AI radically reduces unit economics, making previously unaffordable services—like bespoke financial advice—accessible to everyone.

FedEx’s Three-Layer AI Strategy and Supply Chain Orchestration

  • Layer 1 — Network Efficiency: Raj Subramaniam explained that FedEx uses deep learning models to optimize a physical network spanning 700 planes, 200,000 trucks, and 5,000 facilities across more than 220 countries and territories.
  • Layer 2 — Customer Differentiation & Predictability: FedEx uses AI to shift from reporting what happened yesterday to predicting tomorrow.
    • Predictive Maintenance: FedEx’s Mobius system identifies equipment issues before they fail across its 5,000 facilities.
    • Healthcare Leadership: Predictive scheduling has helped FedEx become the largest express transportation provider for healthcare over the last three to four years.
  • Layer 3 — Global Supply Chain Orchestration: With $1.9 trillion in global supply chain inefficiencies, FedEx is leveraging the 2 petabytes of data it generates daily (from 18 million packages connecting 3 million shippers and 300 million consumers) to orchestrate end-to-end supply chains.
    • US Army Partnership: FedEx and Alphabet/Google recently announced a combined deal to streamline the US Army’s supply chain.
  • Model-Agnostic & Unreplicable Moat: Subramaniam stated FedEx is agnostic on open-weight versus closed or Chinese models, focusing instead on its data foundation; he dismissed fears of frontier labs reverse-engineering FedEx’s business, noting its global physical network for high-value commerce (healthcare, aerospace, defense, data centers) is impossible to replicate.

Global Expansion, Earth AI, and Full-Stack Differentiation

  • Emerging Markets & Global South: Following visits to Malaysia and Thailand (Bangkok), Porat emphasized that heads of state view AI as essential for economic transformation.
    • Earth AI & Farmers: Partnering with World Bank President Ajay Banga, Google layered Gemini onto Google Earth to create Earth AI, delivering pre- and post-harvest insights to farmers on Android phones that drive a low double-digit uplift in earnings, alongside crisis alerts for flash flooding and fires.
    • Healthcare Screening: Google’s AI-powered early screening for diabetic retinopathy has completed more than 1 million scans to date.
  • TPUs & Full-Stack Approach: Asked whether Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s departure creates an opening for Google’s TPUs, Porat noted that global demand for TPUs is strong regardless, driven by Alphabet’s decade-long full-stack strategy (research, models, TPUs, and platforms serving over 1 billion people), though Nvidia GPUs remain a strong part of Google’s fleet.

Geopolitics, Tariffs, and Cross-Border Compliance

  • From Basic Math to Advanced Calculus: Subramaniam noted that moving goods across borders has grown far more complex over the past three to four years due to shifting tariff environments, requiring "advanced calculus" that FedEx embeds into customer workflows via AI.
  • Export Controls & Chip Diversion: Addressing concerns about AI chips reaching restricted destinations, Subramaniam stated that FedEx’s digital manifest tracking ensures 100% visibility into what customers declare and that the company strictly screens anything on export control lists.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Diabetic Retinopathy AI Screening: "We've done more than a million scans to date. We're looking to hit 7 million, I presume we'll up it from there." — Ruth Porat, President of Alphabet

Notable Quotes

"Whereas the headlines are the majority don't want a data center, that conversation flips it to the majority do want us to build because we're doing it the right way with the commitment that we're going to do it the right way." — Ruth Porat
"Just three, four years ago basic math would have done. Now you need advanced calculus, and we do advanced calculus." — Raj Subramaniam
"AI is enabling us to radically reduce unit economics to better deliver for our stakeholders. And when you can do that, things that were previously not affordable, not available, are now addressable." — Ruth Porat
Ruth Porat of Alphabet and Semafor's Liz Hoffman. Morning. Morning. Thanks for joining us. Ruth we'll start with you. We just came out of an election cycle where AI did not do well. Voters do not like this and I'm curious whether you think it is a failure of storytelling or of execution and how Google is going to move forward in its it's very big AI build out. Fmr. Commerce Sec. Gina Raimondo & Hyundai CEO José Muñoz at Silicon Valley & The World | Semafor

Semafor(37.4K)·19:51·359 views·2026-11-13
Gina RaimondoFormer Commerce Secretary José MuñozCEO, Hyundai Shelly BanjoDeputy Editor-in-Chief, Semafor
TL;DRFormer Commerce Secretary Gina Raimondo criticized transactional US chip export policies while introducing RAISE, her $750M non-profit aimed at preventing an AI-driven labor crisis, as Hyundai CEO José Muñoz detailed his company's $26B US investment and pivot into "physical AI" with Waymo, Uber, and Boston Dynamics.
National Security vs. Revenue: Former Commerce Secretary Gina Raimondo stated that export controls must prioritize national security without putting a price tag on it, criticizing the current administration's reported approach of allowing chip sales in exchange for a 25% cut. ...

TL;DR: Former Commerce Secretary Gina Raimondo criticized transactional US chip export policies while introducing RAISE, her $750M non-profit aimed at preventing an AI-driven labor crisis, as Hyundai CEO José Muñoz detailed his company's $26B US investment and pivot into "physical AI" with Waymo, Uber, and Boston Dynamics.

US-China Export Controls & Chip Policy

  • National Security vs. Revenue: Former Commerce Secretary Gina Raimondo stated that export controls must prioritize national security without putting a price tag on it, criticizing the current administration's reported approach of allowing chip sales in exchange for a 25% cut.
    • During her tenure, she worked closely with chipmakers—including Jensen Huang, Lisa Su, and Cristiano Amon ("Christian")—to set clear "rules of the road" while minimizing lost revenue.
  • Cracking Down on Compute Rental: Raimondo noted that the administration issued new rules to block China from renting compute (such as accessing Blackwell chips in foreign data centers).
    • She expressed frustration that it took two years to finalize, noting she had started that work before leaving office.
  • Geopolitical Stakes for South Korea: Hyundai CEO José Muñoz emphasized that South Korea is the US's "number one ally" and heavily reliant on chip supply for companies like Samsung and SK.
    • Hyundai is a major GPU consumer for its robotics and autonomous driving initiatives and hopes Nvidia's work continues smoothly regardless of leadership changes.

Hyundai’s Physical AI Pivot & US Investment

  • From Automaker to "Physical AI" Company: Muñoz described Hyundai as a tech and physical AI company, leveraging Boston Dynamics for robotics and Motional for autonomous driving.
  • Robotaxi Rollouts:
    • Waymo: Hyundai is beginning to deliver robotaxis to Waymo this month, with plans to export significant volumes, including to right-hand drive markets.
    • Uber & Motional: Hyundai is launching a robotaxi service on Uber's app using Motional vehicles produced in Savannah, Georgia.
  • Massive US Capital Commitment: Muñoz stated Hyundai is the largest American OEM by investment, committing $26 billion in the US over the next two years—compared to $20.4 billion invested over the previous 40 years.
    • He noted that a strong automotive foundation funds these new technologies, giving Hyundai a competitive edge against Chinese rivals.

Preparing the Workforce for the AI Economy (RAISE)

  • The Weakest Link in AI: Raimondo argued that the biggest threat to AI's future is not compute, talent, or energy, but a messy labor transition that puts millions out of work and triggers a regulatory backlash.
    • She pointed to an election two weeks prior where more than 45 data center moratoria were on the ballot, and all but one passed due to public anxiety over jobs.
  • RAISE Mission & Funding: Raimondo is full-time CEO, chair, and founder of RAISE, a non-partisan non-profit that has raised $750 million ("three quarters of a billion dollars") to modernize US workforce systems.
    • Corporate Supporters: Working with dozens of companies, including Apple, AT&T, and Amazon; tech company funding accounts for less than half of the total, and donors have no board governance.
    • Policy Reform: Partnering with both Democratic and Republican governors to fix unemployment insurance—currently, laid-off workers lose benefits if they start a business or go back to school—and to incentivize corporate redeployment over "fire-rehire."
  • Personal Motivation & 2028: Raimondo dismissed running for president in 2028, committing to RAISE and citing her childhood experience when her father's jewelry factory job was shipped to China.

Corporate Governance & Navigating the Labor Transition

  • Job Creation Over Displacement: Muñoz argued that robotics and autonomous driving empower workers rather than replace them, comparing the shift to the expansion of TV channels and streaming services increasing overall volume.
    • Hyundai is actively hiring higher-qualified technicians and engineers in places like Savannah to train and maintain robots.
  • Protecting the Workforce: Muñoz highlighted that Hyundai's 330,000-person "family" did not lay off a single worker during COVID-19, instead adapting operations to protect employees.
  • Board Independence: Addressing questions on corporate oversight, Muñoz noted that Hyundai maintains strict governance with at least 7 out of 12 board members serving as independent directors.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • US Investment: Hyundai will invest $26 billion in the United States over the next two years — *José Muñoz*
  • Waymo Deliveries: Hyundai will begin delivering robotaxis to Waymo this month, expanding into significant export volumes including right-hand drive markets — *José Muñoz*
  • AI Backlash Risk: If the US fails to ensure a prosperous job transition and puts millions out of work, it will trigger an economic recession and regulatory backlash that will stop AI — *Gina Raimondo*

Notable Quotes

"Either it's for America's national security or not. It's not for a price." — *Gina Raimondo*
"If we don't make sure that every American can have a prosperous job in an AI economy and instead we go through a messy transition with millions of people immediately put out of work, that's going to stop AI." — *Gina Raimondo*
"We were supposed to be an automotive group. Now, we are a tech company. We are a physical AI company." — *José Muñoz*
"We produce robots which are kind of superhuman and do things that human beings cannot do. So, we empower the humans to be better." — *José Muñoz*

Former US Treasury Secretary Warns Of ‘Underestimating’ Risk Of AI Financial Crisis

Semafor(37.4K)·18:59·555 views·2026-11-13
Hank PaulsonFormer US Treasury Secretary Rishi SunakFormer UK Prime Minister Gina ChonExecutive Editor, Semafor
TL;DRFormer US Treasury Secretary Hank Paulson and former UK Prime Minister Rishi Sunak warned that while the US leads in AI innovation, China is winning the long game on distribution and domestic adoption—and the West is severely underestimating both the financial bubble risks and the potential for a catastrophic AI accident to trigger a market and regulatory collapse.
Bipolar Global Order: Hank Paulson framed the current landscape as an intense competition between the US and China, each backed by different economic and political models competing for power, prestige, and economic advantage. US Leads Innovation, China Wins Distribution: ...

TL;DR: Former US Treasury Secretary Hank Paulson and former UK Prime Minister Rishi Sunak warned that while the US leads in AI innovation, China is winning the long game on distribution and domestic adoption—and the West is severely underestimating both the financial bubble risks and the potential for a catastrophic AI accident to trigger a market and regulatory collapse.

The US-China AI Race: Innovation vs. Distribution

  • Bipolar Global Order: Hank Paulson framed the current landscape as an intense competition between the US and China, each backed by different economic and political models competing for power, prestige, and economic advantage.
  • US Leads Innovation, China Wins Distribution:
    • Paulson noted that the US remains the center of cutting-edge innovation, driven by companies like Nvidia, OpenAI, and Anthropic.
    • Recognizing they will not lead in frontier innovation, China is executing a long-game "Huawei strategy" focused on giving technology away, training users, and commercializing it through robotics and other products.
    • China is also winning on domestic adoption, whereas US companies face a competitive disadvantage due to the high cost of compute, chips, and enterprise software.

Middle-Power Strategy, Sovereignty, and Public Trust

  • Three Pillars for Middle Powers: Rishi Sunak outlined how countries outside the US and China should approach AI policy:
    • Adoption and Diffusion: Countries do not need to be at the frontier of innovation to use AI to benefit their economies and citizens.
    • Public Trust: Democratic societies cannot diffuse AI broadly unless citizens trust it is being deployed responsibly.
    • Sovereignty: Because middle powers cannot build an entire sovereign tech stack, they must rely on alliances (such as the UK's close relationship with the US) while diversifying options to maintain leverage.
  • Protecting National Champions: Sunak noted that governments will increasingly scrutinize who invests in and controls key domestic firms—such as the UK's DeepMind and ElevenLabs—to preserve leverage in future technology supply chains.
  • The Western Trust Deficit:
    • Public polling shows populations in India, the Gulf, and China are significantly more optimistic about AI than those in the US and Europe (where broader economic angst has fueled protests in France and Germany).
    • Sunak pointed out a silver lining: individuals' personal experiences with AI are much more positive than their abstract fears about its impact on society, giving governments and tech companies a foundation to build on through job-transition support and safety guardrails.

Financial Bubble and Catastrophic Risk

  • Unprecedented Capital Concentration: Drawing on his experience at Goldman Sachs and the US Treasury, Paulson warned he has never seen so much money chasing a single opportunity with so little earnings and ROI backing it up.
  • Underestimating Tail Risks:
    • Paulson argued that investors and executives are brushing aside catastrophic risks, noting that multiple AI accidents have already occurred even as companies push forward to go public.
    • He warned that US capital markets—while the best in the world—were not built for the AI boom and lack the necessary checks and balances.
  • The Consequence of a Major Failure: Both Paulson and Sunak agreed that a single catastrophic AI accident would trigger a severe public and regulatory overreaction that could shut down or drastically slow development.

Governance, National Security, and US-China Dialogue

  • A Middle Way on Regulation:
    • Sunak criticized the EU AI Act as overly complicated and an attempt to legislate every conceivable use case—a misstep he noted Mario Draghi has publicly acknowledged as Europe looks to roll parts of it back.
    • Instead, both speakers advocated for industry-government collaboration modeled on the President's Working Group on Capital Markets and Jay Clayton's Super Intelligence Task Force.
    • Core Governance Mechanisms: Sunak called for industry-defined common standards, independent third-party audits ("you can't have companies marking their own homework"), narrow focus on high-risk domains (cyber, bio, chemical), and financial-style incident reporting for near misses.
  • Lowered Barriers for Bad Actors:
    • Gina Chon and Sunak highlighted that AI makes it cheaper and easier for non-state actors (such as the Houthis) and adversaries in ongoing conflicts (Ukraine, Iran) to cause catastrophic harm.
    • Sunak pointed to the UK's newly established AI threat assessment center and AI Security Institute as models for synthesizing intelligence and hardening critical infrastructure defenses.
  • US-China Safety Cooperation:
    • Paulson revealed that an op-ed he wrote on existential AI risk reached China's Politburo and drew an official response, indicating that Chinese leaders share concerns about catastrophic AI risk.
    • He urged the US and China to establish a crisis hotline to contain potential catastrophic events, despite a massive bilateral trust deficit.
    • Sunak cited the UK's decision to invite China to the first AI Safety Summit at Bletchley Park—where China engaged and signed the declaration—as proof that dialogue is worthwhile, while Paulson highlighted upcoming US-China AI talks led by Scott Bessent as an important first step.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Middle-Power Intervention: "You're going to increasingly see middle powers, I think governments take a little bit more interest in those companies that they think can provide that national champion leverage for them in the technology supply chains of the future." — Rishi Sunak
  • Catastrophic Risk Shutdown: "One catastrophic accident would shut it down." — Hank Paulson

Notable Quotes

"I've spent a lot of time looking at risk at Goldman Sachs and at Treasury, and I've never seen so much money chasing one opportunity with not a lot of earnings and ROI backing it up." — Hank Paulson
"The problem people have with risk is never not identifying the risk, but it's not giving enough weight to the big risk." — Hank Paulson
"You can't have companies marking their own homework. I think that the average person would think that's reasonable and it's good that people are talking about independent audits and evaluations." — Rishi Sunak
"Whereas before you could keep a track of relatively large state actors who could cause you enormous amount of harm, this makes it much easier for a far greater number of smaller actors to do similar catastrophic damage potentially." — Rishi Sunak

How Mozilla uses GPT-6 Cyber to find and fix Firefox vulnerabilities

OpenAI(2.1M)·20:19·13.5K views·2026-11-13
Brian GrinsteadSecurity Engineer, Mozilla Clint GiblerCybersecurity Lead, OpenAI
TL;DRBy combining decades of browser security preparation with GPT-6 Cyber agents, Mozilla scaled its bug discovery and fix rate by 8× from 2025 to 2026, successfully preventing any successful exploits at the annual Pwn2Own competition.
Pre-Competition Scramble: Browsers remain a top target for the annual Pwn2Own hacking competition, which Mozilla uses as a real-world fire drill. OpenAI Collaboration: Shortly before the 2026 event, the OpenAI cyber team filed a few bugs; after Mozilla quickly fixed and...

TL;DR

By combining decades of browser security preparation with GPT-6 Cyber agents, Mozilla scaled its bug discovery and fix rate by 8× from 2025 to 2026, successfully preventing any successful exploits at the annual Pwn2Own competition.

1. Pwn2Own Fire Drill and Agentic Partnerships

  • Pre-Competition Scramble: Browsers remain a top target for the annual Pwn2Own hacking competition, which Mozilla uses as a real-world fire drill.
    • OpenAI Collaboration: Shortly before the 2026 event, the OpenAI cyber team filed a few bugs; after Mozilla quickly fixed and uplifted them, it was discovered that a number of researcher submissions were attempting to use those exact bugs.
    • Event Outcome: Mozilla successfully defended Firefox, with no participants able to compromise the browser.

2. Decades of Foundational Security Preparation

  • Prerequisites for AI Integration: Mozilla’s ability to successfully adopt an agentic tool chain was built on years of foundational security investments.
    • External Bug Bounties: Decades of running an external bug bounty program established comfort with receiving a high volume of external reports.
    • Internal Tooling: Long-standing adoption of address sanitizers, fuzzers, debuggable builds, and internal red teams provided the exact primitives agents now use to spin up Firefox instances, test potential exploits, and iterate rapidly.

3. Agentic Security Pipeline & Inner Loop Architecture

  • Intelligent File Prioritization: Alphabetical file-tree scanning proved ineffective, leading Mozilla to use an LLM-based grading system incorporating technical documentation, researcher artifacts, and historical risk scores to generate target lists.
  • Inner Loop Execution:
    • VM Isolation & Goals: An agent is placed inside its own VM with access to the Firefox source code and fuzzing tool chain, tasked with finding bugs via static analysis, custom mini-fuzzers, and dynamic testing.
    • Deterministic Verification: The agent must produce deterministic proof (such as an HTML file causing a crash in Firefox) to exit the inner loop.
  • Secondary Judging & Quality Control:
    • A second grading agent checks the work to catch anomalies (such as an agent artificially patching the codebase to force a crash) and grade severity against Mozilla's threat model, resulting in a tiny false positive rate.
    • Auto-Fix Generation: Within the inner loop, an agent generates a code patch and a test case to accompany the bug report.

4. Scaling Triage, Deduplication, and Variant Analysis

  • Automated Deduplication Challenges: Distinguishing unique vulnerabilities from hundreds of scan findings proved unintuitively difficult due to nuanced crash stack variations.
    • Database Ingestion: Mozilla ingests all findings into a local database and tasks an agent with connecting dots under strict guidance.
    • False Duplicate Safeguards: If a finding is marked as a duplicate, the system creates a reminder to confirm it no longer works once the primary bug lands in the tree.
  • Severity Re-Evaluation via GPT-6 Cyber: Powerful cyber models uncovered that certain bugs originally graded as moderate/low severity actually bypassed architectural hardening and qualified as security-high, providing vital feedback to improve defense-in-depth.
  • Broad Variant and Commit Scanning:
    • Variant Analysis: Bundling known security bugs fixed in 2025 and tasking agents to hunt for similar patterns across the codebase yielded a majority of findings by September, generating fewer duplicate reports.
    • Commit Scanning: Narrowly inspecting new commits for potential vulnerabilities works alongside broad variant scans as part of the core strategy.

5. Engineer Workflows and 8× Metric Scaling

  • Assisting Human Engineers: While generated fix patches are rarely landable as-is due to narrow targeting or missing codebase context, expert browser engineers use them alongside descriptions and evidence to review and land fixes.
  • Massive Productivity Gains: Across its security workflow, Mozilla achieved an 8× increase in bug discovery and fixes from 2025 to 2026.
  • Agentic Vulnerability Workbench:
    • SQLite Ingestion: Signals from all runs, bugs, and engineer comments are ingested into a local SQLite file on red team engineers' machines.
    • Workflow Queries: Coding agents can run SQL queries to plan next scanning patches based on Slack hints or review raw traces and `blockers.yaml` files to fix environment dependencies.

Recursive self-improvement has started

Dwarkesh Patel(1.5M)·44:27·386.1K views·2026-11-13
Dwarkesh PatelHost, Dwarkesh Patel
TL;DRAI is already accelerating its own R&D, making models that rival the best human researchers globally plausibly as early as 2027—sparking a recursive self-improvement loop that could yield superintelligence within years.
Current Acceleration at Frontiers: Independent evaluation organizations and frontier labs report dramatic internal speedups driven by AI use. Meter's Assessment: Found Anthropic's rate of AI progress on well-scoped tasks doubled over a year, with the "majority of this...

TL;DR

AI is already accelerating its own R&D, making models that rival the best human researchers globally plausibly as early as 2027—sparking a recursive self-improvement loop that could yield superintelligence within years.

AI Research Acceleration & The Mathematics Parallel

  • Current Acceleration at Frontiers: Independent evaluation organizations and frontier labs report dramatic internal speedups driven by AI use.
      • Meter's Assessment: Found Anthropic's rate of AI progress on well-scoped tasks doubled over a year, with the "majority of this acceleration" attributable to AI-driven R&D.
      • OpenAI & Anthropic Metrics: OpenAI reported that its heaviest 10% of users scaled internal coding agent spending from $1,000/day to over $7,000/day between early summer and August; Anthropic reported Claude leading 26% of its AI R&D work by September (up from <1% in February).
  • Explosive Progress in Mathematics: AI models have sliced through verifiable math domains, turning complex open problems into routine side quests.
      • Timeline of Capability: Models advanced from failing high school math competitions in 2024 to winning IMO gold medals in 2025, and ultimately solving a Millennium Prize problem in 88 hours.
      • Verifiability Advantage: While math progress focuses heavily on verification (e.g., finding counterexamples) rather than broad theory, AI labs prioritize empirical algorithmic recipes over theory, making AI research a natural fit for this capability.
  • The Transformer Analogy: Breakthroughs like the Transformer architecture—built on known components like attention and residual networks—represent the exact type of systematic idea-matching and small-scale experimentation at which AI models excel.

Dynamics of the Intelligence Explosion

  • Three Drivers of Algorithmic Speedup: Once AIs match human AI researchers, progress will surge due to speed, intelligence, and scale.
      • Speed: Current AIs operate at 10x human speed around the clock, allowing individual automated researchers to complete roughly 50x more work per calendar week than a human.
      • Intelligence: Every automated researcher will match the caliber of the top 50 foundational researchers who typically drive 80% of algorithmic progress at a frontier lab.
      • Scale: Unlike tasks requiring a singular voice (like writing a novel), AI research features modular subtasks and verifiable proxies, enabling massive parallelization (swarms trying thousands of approaches simultaneously).
  • Two Divergent Takeoff Scenarios: The net trajectory depends on whether recursive self-improvement outpaces the increasing difficulty of finding new ideas.
      • Software-Only Singularity (50% probability): AIs rapidly iterate smarter versions of themselves within months, scraping against physical hardware limits.
      • The Spark (50% probability): Achieves 3 to 6 years of progress in a single calendar year before stabilizing while humanity builds more compute.

Real-World Generalization & Data Ownership

  • Scaling RL for General Skills: While data centers cannot directly simulate real-world environments like courtroom litigation or business founding, massive reinforcement learning (RL) in hyper-realistic virtual worlds will forge general-purpose learners.
      • Virtual Training Environments: AIs will navigate complex, multi-century corporate competition simulations, historical back-tests (e.g., predicting the Cuban Missile Crisis or deriving General Relativity from pre-1700 data), and algorithmic self-improvement.
      • Evolutionary Precedent: Natural evolution built a general-purpose human learner using a crude verifier (survival/reproduction) on the savanna; deliberate RL optimization will yield far superior transfer capabilities.
  • Rapid Deployment Ramp-Up: Post-spark AIs will bridge the real-world experience gap within months of deployment—mirroring young historical prodigies like 27-year-old Napoleon or 25-year-old Rockefeller—while digesting enterprise data (emails, Slack messages, employee interviews) in days.
  • Synthetic Data Generation: Data scarcity will be overcome as automated systems construct massive, high-quality synthetic datasets and RL environments using difficulty and correctness as reward signals.

Verifiable R&D Proxies & Overcoming Objections

  • The Three Buckets of Verifiable AI R&D: Algorithmic progress is trackable via clear internal metrics across pre-training, post-training, and inference.
      • Pre-Training: Minimized compute required to reach fixed pre-training loss on nano-GPT benchmarks, alongside scaling law predictions (e.g., Magic's model matching DeepSeek V4 Pro for half the compute of original GPT-3, roughly $500,000).
      • Post-Training: Measured reductions in compute/data/time required for new RL environments, evaluation on unseen tasks (e.g., Bytedance's *Edgebench*), and internal historical replays (e.g., Anthropic's *Coobch* benchmark).
      • Inference Efficiency: Directly measurable metrics including GPU utilization, KV cache size per token, total forward-pass flops, and latency.
  • Addressing Key Bottlenecks:
      • Continual Learning: Solved either via extended in-context learning windows or online learning research problems possessing clean verifiable proxies; even amnesiac models can solve Millennium Prize problems without long-term memory.
      • Research Taste & Proxies: Paradigm-agnostic outer-loop proxies (e.g., solving millennium problems or discovering physical laws from historical data) bridge paradigm shifts.
      • Experiment Compute Constraints: Frontier compute scaling (quadrupling yearly) will provide thousands of agents with sufficient hourly compute to run GPT-3 scale experiments, replacing scarce human researcher time with compute-heavy ablation pipelines.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Dwarkesh Patel: We are only a couple of years away—and possibly only a couple of months away—from AIs that are as good as the best humans at AI research.
  • Dwarkesh Patel: By 2030, and plausibly as early as next year, we will have AIs that are at least as good as the best human experts in every field.
  • Dwarkesh Patel: The median year for when automated researchers will be as good as the very best human AI researchers at doing AI research is 2028 (assuming full-steam-ahead development without intentional slowdowns or government intervention).
  • Dwarkesh Patel: There is at least a 25% chance that automating AI-level researchers will not happen until after 2032 due to persistent difficulties like continual learning.
  • Dwarkesh Patel: There is a real possibility that AI capabilities reach this milestone as early as 2027, supported by straightforward takeoffs models fed by Anthropic's reported doubling of progress rates.
  • Dwarkesh Patel: There is a 50% probability of a software-only singularity and a 50% probability of a spark scenario (compressing 3 to 6 years of progress into a single calendar year).

Notable Quotes

"If you take at face value that AI is now nearly doubling the rate of AI capabilities progress and assume that acceleration from AI is smooth, this naively implies that we would reach extreme super intelligence within a year." — *AJ Coocher (cited by Dwarkesh Patel)*
"And there is one sure thing about the fall of gods: they do not fall a little, they crash and shatter." — *Dwarkesh Patel (quoting John Steinbeck's East of Eden regarding math progress)*
