State Secretary Marco Rubio delivers keynote address at National Lawyers Convention — 11/13/26

CNBC Television(3.4M)·60:55·2026-11-13
Marco RubioU.S. Secretary of State Danielle RubioLaw student, University of Virginia
TL;DRSpeaking at the Federalist Society’s 2026 National Lawyers Convention, U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio argued that lasting political and legal change requires building institutions over decades rather than just winning elections, while defending an "America First" foreign policy that rejects the authority of international bodies like the International Criminal Court and prioritizes preventing Iran from acquiring a nuclear weapon.
Biographical Overview: Danielle Rubio, a third-year law student at the University of Virginia and daughter of Marco Rubio, introduced her father, noting he was born in Miami, Florida, in 1971 to Cuban immigrants. Political & Senate Career: After working as a land use and zoning...

TL;DR: Speaking at the Federalist Society’s 2026 National Lawyers Convention, U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio argued that lasting political and legal change requires building institutions over decades rather than just winning elections, while defending an "America First" foreign policy that rejects the authority of international bodies like the International Criminal Court and prioritizes preventing Iran from acquiring a nuclear weapon.

Background & Introduction

  • Biographical Overview: Danielle Rubio, a third-year law student at the University of Virginia and daughter of Marco Rubio, introduced her father, noting he was born in Miami, Florida, in 1971 to Cuban immigrants.
  • Political & Senate Career: After working as a land use and zoning attorney, city commissioner in West Miami, and Speaker of the Florida House, Rubio served nearly 14 years in the U.S. Senate, where he led efforts on the Uyghur Forced Labor Prevention Act, Hong Kong democracy legislation, and pressure on the Cuban regime.
  • Cabinet Appointment: Nominated by President Donald Trump in November 2024, Rubio was unanimously confirmed by the Senate 99–0 on January 20, 2025, and sworn in on January 21, 2025, as the 72nd Secretary of State and National Security Advisor.

Institutional Power & Lessons from the Federalist Society

  • The 1982 Conservative Crisis: Rubio drew parallels to early 1982, when President Ronald Reagan’s approval rating fell 25 points from 68% to 43% by March 1982—four points below Jimmy Carter at the same point—amid a recession and unemployment approaching double digits (the worst in 40 years).
  • Origins of the Federalist Society: Against that backdrop in April 1982, four law students organized a federalism symposium in New Haven, Connecticut, attended by roughly 200 people, where 46-year-old professor Antonin Scalia diagnosed that conservatives had been "outgunned" federally for half a century despite Republicans winning three of the last four presidential elections and appointing seven of nine Supreme Court justices.
  • Beyond Elections: Rubio emphasized that winning elections only determines who holds office for a season, whereas true political power resides in universities, the press, NGOs, corporate boardrooms, and think tanks.
      • He credited the Federalist Society with understanding that lasting transformation takes a generation or more, built by recruiting young talent into pipelines of power rather than relying on two-, four-, or eight-year election cycles.

International Law, Sovereignty, & American Identity

  • Rejection of "Weaponized" International Law: Rubio argued that global elites and unelected bureaucrats use international law as an instrument to weaken U.S. sovereignty, citing criticisms of U.S. military strikes on cartel boats in the Caribbean, the capture of Nicolas Maduro, the 2022 overturning of *Roe v. Wade*, and ACLU petitions to the UN regarding U.S. immigration enforcement.
  • Sanctioning the ICC: Rubio defended President Trump’s decision to sanction the International Criminal Court (ICC), calling it a "fake" and unaccountable tribunal that attempts to supersede sovereign constitutions.
  • American Expansion & Character: Rejecting what he called the "spirit of Marx" that seeks to abolish distinctions between nations, Rubio highlighted America's historic ambition—growing from 13 states in 1776 to a continental empire of nearly 3 million square miles by 1848 (just 72 years later).
  • Assimilation & Citizenship: Responding to Harvard Law 2L Tasha Dambacher, Rubio distinguished between coming to live and work in America versus coming to *be* an American, noting that "Americans by choice" who immigrate legally often possess a deeper appreciation for U.S. freedoms than native-born citizens.

Foreign Policy, Economics, & The Iran Conflict

  • Putting America First: Answering University of Texas student Mariah Sraom, Rubio stated that the U.S. government's primary duty is to work for Americans, noting he had replaced international flags at the State Department with American flags for "America 250." He added that he and President Trump will travel next week to the Philippines for the ASEAN conference and to China for the APEC conference.
  • The Iran Threat: Responding to William & Mary Law 2L Lucy Griffin on what constitutes an American victory in Iran, Rubio stated that Iran's radical Shia clerical regime has waged war on the U.S. since 1979 through proxies—citing Hezbollah's 1982–83 barracks bombing that killed over 280 U.S. Marines, the early 1990s bombing of a Jewish center in Buenos Aires, militias in Iraq, the Syrian civil war, and Houthi attacks.
  • Defining Victory: Rubio defined victory as ensuring Iran *never* acquires a nuclear weapon. He argued that current U.S. economic pressure is severely weakening the regime without requiring direct military action, noting that while Iran shoots at ships in the Strait of Hormuz, 13 to 14 million barrels of oil still pass through every night.

Q&A: Faith, Youth, & Economic Reality

  • Faith in the Legal Profession: Answering Florida State College of Law 3L Morgan Hughes, Rubio stated that faith is "who you are," not just what you do, and argued that Judeo-Christian values of human dignity underpin the U.S. criminal justice system's presumption of innocence.
  • Addressing Youth Anxiety & Economics: Responding to Berkeley Law 3L Schnair Gansburg on why young Americans are drawn to political extremes, Rubio acknowledged that younger people today are financially further behind than their peers were 20, 30, or 40 years ago, and that most Americans judge the economy by their ability to afford daily needs rather than the stock market. He argued that the free market is a tool that must serve people and provide dignified work, not the other way around.

Notable Quotes

"Elections decide who holds office for a season. But elections do not decide by themselves what our institutions teach or what our lawyers believe, which ideas are treated as respectable or normal and which are deemed beyond the pale." — Marco Rubio
"If they're foolish enough to try to enforce their paper decrees on Americans, our answer will probably be the same as the Roman general Pompey when he said to those who were trying to invoke their city's charters against his legions: stop quoting laws to men with swords." — Marco Rubio
"I believe in the free market, but I don't worship the free market. I think the market exists to serve people, not people to serve the market." — Marco Rubio
"Victory is ensuring that they never have a nuclear weapon... They have killed tens and tens of thousands of their own people, slaughtered them in the streets... Imagine what they would do to us if they had a nuclear weapon." — Marco Rubio, on Iran
Born in Miami, Florida, in 1971, he is the son of Cuban immigrants who left Cuba in pursuit of the American dream. His father worked as a banquet bartender while his mother split time as a stay-at-home mom and hotel maid. From an early age, Rubio learned the importance of faith, family, community, and dignified work. His commitment to public service was deeply influenced by conversations with his grandfather, who had witnessed firsthand the devastating effects of communism on his homeland. Inspired by his family story and his belief in the promise of America, Rubio embarked on a career in public service that would take him from local government to the highest levels of national leadership. After marrying his wife, Jeanette, Rubio began his political career as a city commissioner in West Miami before serving in the Florida House of Representatives, where he ultimately rose to become speaker. In 2010, he was elected to represent Florida in the United States Senate, where he served for nearly 14 years. During his tenure in the Senate, Rubio served on several influential committees, including the Senate Foreign Relations Committee, the Senate Select Committee on Intelligence, the Senate Appropriations Committee, and the Senate Committee on Small Business and Entrepreneurship. He became particularly active on issues involving US foreign policy, national security, and America's relationships with countries across the world. Among his legislative accomplishments, Rubio was a leading force behind the Uyghur Forced Labor Prevention Act, co-led legislation supporting human rights and democracy in Hong Kong, and worked with President Trump to increase pressure on the Cuban regime, including efforts targeting the regime's military-controlled business empire. In November 2024, President Donald Trump nominated Rubio to serve as the 72nd Secretary of State. On January 20th, 2025, the United States unanimously confirmed his nomination by a vote of 99 to zero, making him the first member of President Trump's second cabinet. Rubio was sworn in as Secretary of State on January 21st, 2025. As America's chief diplomat, he leads the Department of State in carrying out the President's foreign policy and advancing Americans' interests around the world. His stated priority is to lead a department that puts America first. In his capacity as the President's National Security Advisor, he leads the National Security Council and coordinates the America First foreign policy across the interagency. And while all of these professional achievements are truly remarkable, his greatest accomplishment cannot be measured by titles, awards, or accolades. Beyond all that he has achieved professionally, his greatest legacy will always be the values he has instilled, including the importance of always putting the Lord first. And this is the example that he has set for my family, along with his love for a great nation and the defense of our basic rights. And for that reason, more than any other, I am most proud and most grateful and most fortunate to call him dad. Now, without further ado, please join me in welcoming the 72nd Secretary of State of the United States, my father, Marco Rubio. Thank you. Thank you very much. Thank you. Is that cool or what? You know what I mean? Huh? My kids, I used to bring them on stage and show them off, and now they're introducing. I don't know where she got this; that's no stage fright. She's not afraid to speak. I mean, I don't really like public speaking that much, but she, on the other hand, that's fantastic. That's great. Thank you, Danielle. You did very good. She's a third-year law student at the University of Virginia. I heard that's a good law school; I hope it is. It's an expensive law school, so I hope it is. No, she's great. And of course, my wife Jeanette is here as well. And you know, I was also a lawyer, but I was a different kind of lawyer. I was a land use and zoning attorney. And I always tell them as a land use and zoning attorney, if you ever wind up in court, something went horribly wrong. She, on the other hand, is going to be like a real lawyer. So she's fantastic, and it's an honor to be with all of you here today. And you know, primarily I am here in this collection of lawyers. How many lawyers—are there any lawyers here? Good, thank you, because on election night there was a congressman that said we all needed to lawyer up, so here we are. Anyway, on a serious topic, less than two years into Ronald Reagan's first term in office, everything sort of seemed headed in the wrong direction. It's hard for us to remember that right now, but that's what it was like after his first two years in office. Things seemed headed in the wrong direction. He had won a stunning landslide victory, which had been hailed as a conservative revolution and upended the political establishment. He'd been swept into office with this ambitious political mandate, he survived an assassination attempt, and he promptly delivered on a signature campaign promise, which was a historic tax cut. And he did all of this in the first eight months of his presidency. But by early 1982—which by the way, I think I was in sixth grade—celebrations had given way to a growing sense of gloom. The Reagan administration was facing its first major political crisis. In less than a year, the approval ratings of the Reagan administration had plummeted by 25 points, from 68% down to 43% by March of 1982. Think about what I'm about to tell you for a moment. By March of 1982, President Reagan was four points less popular than Jimmy Carter at the same point in his term. The economy had taken a nosedive; in fact, it faced a full-fledged recession. Unemployment was approaching double digits, the worst it had been in 40 years, with many fairly or unfairly blaming the president for all of this. Even his foreign policy ambitions were beginning to lose ground, with rising fears of nuclear war with Russia spilling out into a popular revolt against his efforts to take on Soviet communism around the world. On all fronts, the Reagan administration and the conservative movement at that time were on the defensive. But it was against that backdrop that I just described to you in 1982—which by the way also culminated in devastating losses for the president's party in Congress—amid the plummeting morale, the anxiety, the despair, that something new began to stir. And it was at that time a little more than just an idea, germinating in just a few law schools, in the common rooms and lecture halls. But soon what was stirring would remake the whole landscape of American politics and law, forever changing the course of the nation. In April of 1982, in the middle of everything I just described to you, there were four law school students that organized a symposium on federalism in New Haven, Connecticut. About 200 people came. One of the speakers that came was this 46-year-old professor from Chicago. His name was Antonin Scalia. And he just diagnosed a basic problem to the room. He said conservatives have simply been outgunned at the federal level for half a century. And of course, nowhere was that more obvious at the time than in the courts. Republicans had won three of the last four presidential elections. Seven of the nine justices at the time on the Supreme Court had been appointed by Republican presidents. And yet none of it seemed to matter much at all. Nine years earlier, the court had delivered its ruling in Roe v. Wade. Five of the seven votes for the decision were Republican appointees. The majority opinion was written by a justice who had been nominated by President Richard Nixon. The most consistent conservative on that court was William Rehnquist, and he spent much of his time as the lone voice of dissent, so much so that his law clerks gifted him a doll of the Lone Ranger. At the time, only one major law school in the country had a conservative student journal. By the way, it happened to be Harvard; it had been founded four years earlier. And the very term originalism itself was only two years old, and it wasn't coined by an originalist. It was coined by a liberal Stanford professor in an article written to criticize and refute the concept. Just a few years later, a sitting justice on the Supreme Court would dismiss this idea of originalism. He would dismiss it as, quote, "little more than arrogance cloaked as humility," sneering that its advocates were persons who have no familiarity with the historical record. This was how the Federalist Society began. But your critics aren't sneering anymore. The Federalist Society helped to change and advance America, and there are many lessons for us to learn today from that success. One of those lessons is that politics requires more than simply winning elections, because the government is not the only source of political power. Power also lives in the universities. Power lives in the press. Power lives in the whole expanse of civil society, all of the institutions that define and shape our understanding of ourselves as a nation and as a people. Every society, and I mean every society, has an elite, has a ruling class. And in ours, that class resides not just in the White House, not just in Congress or the courts. It also resides in academia, in powerful nonprofits, and in non-government organizations. It resides in corporate boardrooms and C-suites, in think tanks and journals, in foundations, and in mass media, stretching across the commanding heights of business and education and culture. This is something that the left over the last six decades has frankly often understood better than the right. Elections decide who holds office for a season. But elections do not decide by themselves what our institutions teach or what our lawyers believe, which ideas are treated as respectable or normal and which are deemed beyond the pale. The government of course can and does play a critical role in shaping these things. But the government can't fundamentally transform them in just two, four, or eight years. That kind of change is implemented over the course of a generation or more, and even then, only through the dedicated work of a coalition of allies across institutions working together both inside and outside of the state. The Federalist Society's victories were built on this insight. It's not just about placing good judges in positions to interpret the law. It's about shaping the world in which they do so, about shaping the norms and the values, the ideas and beliefs, the expectations and the assumptions that underpin how they think and reason and act. Who defines these things and to what end? That is what power is. That's not unique to our country's legal system. At the State Department, we have seen the same thing firsthand in the realm of what is called international law. We've heard the concept international law invoked against nearly every major foreign policy decision or action of this administration. So, for example, when President Trump ordered our military to sink cartel boats in the Caribbean, we were told that they were violating international law. The founding chief prosecutor of the fake and illegitimate International Criminal Court even accused us of crimes against humanity. When American forces captured Nicolas Maduro, a fugitive of American justice, and brought him to the United States to face that justice, we heard the same accusations from the highest levels of the United Nations. Even the enforcement of our own immigration laws here on American soil has faced this attack. Earlier this year, for example, groups like the ACLU were petitioning the United Nations to investigate alleged violations of international law here in the United States. Over the past decade, we've received an endless barrage of claims about America's alleged violations of international rules and norms, ranging from our foreign policy to our border enforcement to even what they call our systemic racism. Even on the issue of abortion, for example, when Roe v. Wade was overturned by the Supreme Court in 2022, it was swiftly denounced by a cadre of, you guessed it, UN human rights experts—an oxymoron—as a betrayal of America's binding legal obligations under international law. None of this has anything to do with any real higher standard of justice. It is a function of the people who control the official organs of global governance. It is the function of the sprawling network of NGOs and agencies staffed by international activists and bureaucrats, along with their patrons here in the United States and around the world. For this global elite, international law is not a neutral body of rules. International law is an instrument, an instrument to advance their interests under the pretense of universal principles. For example, they're staunch defenders of democracy until particular elections challenge their interests. They lecture us on the importance of sovereignty even as they demand that nations surrender control of their borders and outsource their immigration laws to unelected international courts. They oppose, they say, all forms of imperialism, of conquest, of domination, but they seek to exercise authority over every country and every government on earth. That's why earlier this year President Trump made the unprecedented decision to sanction the fake International Criminal Court. A fake court which is an unelected world tribunal which claims to supersede the laws and constitutions of sovereign states, even those who never signed up for it. Americans have never agreed to submit to the ICC's authority. But that court and its allies in the global bureaucracy have appointed themselves judge and jury and executioner of the international order, ruling over all, but accountable to no one. Armed with the power to investigate, to prosecute, to persecute, and to jail our citizens according to laws we neither consent to nor control. They can certainly try, I would say, but if they're foolish enough to try to enforce their paper decrees on Americans, our answer will probably be the same as the Roman general Pompey when he said to those who were trying to invoke their city's charters against his legions: "Stop quoting laws to men with swords." Not politically correct to say that, but that's what he said. The weaponization of international law is not altogether different from what we've seen here at home. The activist courts who seek to govern by injunction, twisting the law to fit whatever their preferences happen to be at any given place or time. Their allies in the academy, in the press, the professional activist class—the same people who just a few years earlier were denouncing the Constitution. These are the same people who just a few years ago were denouncing the Constitution as a racist and backwards document that was written by slave owners, designed only to protect the interests of wealthy white men. These same people now wax poetic about how they are defending our principles of government and carrying out their constitutional duties. For these people, the fundamental purpose of law, American law and international law alike, is to weaken the United States. It is to undermine our power as a sovereign nation to act. It is to hinder the American government's ability to defend American interests at home and abroad. They tell us that we are compelled by a sacred legal obligation to give up our power, to surrender our sovereignty, to submit to the whims of our enemies, to allow our cities to descend into violent anarchy, to welcome in any foreigner from anywhere in the world who arrives at our doorstep without permission, to transform our towns into refugee camps for the entire planet. And beyond our borders, too, they would have us believe that we must allow our rivals to act against us with impunity, that those who flood our country with drugs and crime and death should be met with little more than the occasional and polite complaint. There is nothing in our history or the history of any other great nation that requires us to recognize the authority or legitimacy of any of these ridiculous ideas. Quite the opposite. It is not only alien to our political and legal tradition, it is alien to who we are. It is an attack on our essential character as a people, a rejection of the qualities and the instincts that have defined Americans since before the founding itself. America has always, and I believe will always, be a nation of very irrepressible ambition. Ambition which since the very beginning extended far beyond our borders. Low ceilings and small horizons were never our destiny. Even the American Revolution itself was driven in part by our forefathers' anger at British limits on our westward expansion, a grievance cited in the very Declaration of Independence itself. The history of our nation and our people brims with a hunger to expand, to grow, to explore, to conquer, to stake a claim to new frontiers. It can be found, by the way, in the opening paragraph of the Federalist Papers, where Hamilton begins by announcing to his readers that the ratification debate would decide the fate of an empire in many respects the most interesting in the world. We Americans, we are not a passive and inward-looking people. To submit to the sterile vision of radical judges or soulless global bureaucrats would be to betray who we are, to betray ourselves. The real America, the nation that actually exists in the text of our founding documents, that actually exists in the history of our people, in the conduct of the men who built backwater colonies into the greatest superpower on earth, was always destined to go beyond, to be greater and stronger than that which had come before. Our laws, our constitution, our legal norms, and our political traditions developed because of, not in spite of, that fact. This is the true American genius of self-government, the unique and distinctive structure of our Constitution combining remarkable freedom and independence with an equally remarkable strength for collective action. It's the expression of these characteristics in institutions developed to organize and channel our ambition, not to suffocate them. Our laws were written by and for a people who were called upwards and eventually even outwards to the stars. From the very beginning, America was on the move. It was alive, it was dynamic, and it was expanding. A nation advancing across the continent, settling land, building cities, laying roads and railways, subduing the wilderness, and painting a great civilization upon the canvas of a new world. In 1776, our new Republic was 13 states clustered along the eastern coast and a handful of scattered frontier outposts just beyond the Appalachians. In 1848, just 72 years later, the American flag flew over a continental empire spanning nearly 3 million square miles in size, running from the eastern cities of Boston and New York to the ports of San Francisco Bay. So think about it this way: a child that was born the year we declared our independence could have lived to see his or her country stretch from the Atlantic to the Pacific. And as our territory expanded, our horizons only grew. No sooner had we reached the western edge of Oregon than we began building new systems, networks, and technologies that extended out across the world, cutting canals across continents that rewrote the rules of geography and forging satellites and spaceships that reached beyond the boundaries of Earth itself. None of this happened by accident. When our predecessors first arrived in North America, when they first came, there were no factories, there were no railroads, there were no skyscrapers. These things exist because we built them, because we acted. These things exist because we acted without fear or moral doubt and overcame all that which threatened or stood in our way. But today we are told by some that the age of great nations pursuing great destinies is over, that we cannot act, we cannot impose ourselves upon the world, that we must surrender our dreams to the consensus of distant tribunals and submit to our place as merely one nation among the many, that we must accustom ourselves to comfortable and quiet decline. We are told that our determination to act in our own interests on our own terms is an assault on the universal, quote-unquote, rules-based order. For example, under this theory, the President himself cannot execute the foreign policy of the United States, cannot restrict trade or immigration, or even examine the way in which we spend our citizens' tax dollars abroad without facing the fury of international lawyers and jurists and NGOs and UN bureaucrats condemning his threats to global values and norms. All of this at home and abroad is a child of the leveling and pessimistic spirit of the age. It entices us to relinquish responsibility for our own destiny and to relieve ourselves of the burdens of greatness and seek refuge in these glittering generalities of international law. Even as we defeated the institution of Soviet communism, we did not triumph over the spirit of Marx. That spirit of Marx lives on in these new forms and doctrines which would subordinate the national will to the menial consensus of the world. Marxism sought to abolish the distinctions between men; the radicals of our time seek to abolish the distinctions between nations themselves. The ultimate question of our time is whether Americans have a moral right to exist as a distinct nation and a distinct people, and thus to demand a government that defends and pursues our interests above all else. Those who tell us that we are living on stolen land, as an example, that our nation was founded and built upon slavery and genocide and oppression, are telling us clearly that they do not believe that we do. They would have us think that America, and more broadly that the West, are uniquely burdened by guilt, guilt that we can only atone for through total surrender, by opening our borders, by renouncing our laws, by retreating from the world and allowing them to remake our society in their image. We should understand clearly that these are the stakes of our political struggle. We should remember that this is what many in the universities, on the courts, or in the halls of government really believe. As recently as 2021, we had a president telling us that hatred was systemically embedded in our laws and our culture. Today, few if any of those people involved on that side of our political debate have renounced those ideas. Their fundamental beliefs have not changed. And in the coming months, we can expect to hear a lot about their deep concern for the Constitution, the law, the principles and axioms of justice. But that concern seems to only appear when it can be wielded as an obstacle or a weapon to advance their interests. Politics, both here in the United States and on the international stage, frankly, is not a marketplace of ideas where good-faith interlocutors can debate and reason together and the better argument always prevails. Persuasion matters, but not everyone is always persuaded. The lesson of the Federalist Society is that victory requires something beyond good arguments. Victory is secured through institutions and networks and systems fixated on a shared goal, capable of shaping and exercising influence. This organization's long march through the institutions of American law was carried out by men and women who were armed not just with ideas, but with ambitious plans of action, cultivating and recruiting the most promising young conservative minds of their generation, funneling them into the pipelines to power, carrying their cause from the lecture halls to the highest courts in the nation. Those who believe that one setback, one defeat, one bad election spells the end of the Republic should look to you as an example for inspiration. And it is the same lesson, by the way, that is written in the fabric of our country, a lesson written in the fabric of our revolution that gave birth to this nation itself. Too often we tell the story of American independence as some certain triumph, as if Washington and the Continental Army were always fated to prevail. Students of our history know that nothing could be further from the truth. The first year of the Revolution was an almost unbroken line of devastating and humiliating defeats. By December of 1776, General Washington had less than 3,000 men fit for duty—limping, starving, freezing, and sick. And they were in desperate retreat with the greatest empire of the age in hot pursuit. All across the colonies the revolutionary spirit seemed to be in collapse. In fact, something not often featured in the movies and in the documentaries and perhaps even the books about this era, thousands of Americans took part in this and renewed their allegiance to the Crown. That's how bad things were. Men who had cheered the cause of independence only months earlier made their peace with what seemed to be inevitable defeat. That month, Washington wrote a letter to his brother. He said unless something changes soon, "I think the game's pretty near up." And he concluded his letter with this quote: "You at a distance can form no idea of the perplexity of my situation. No man, I believe, ever had a greater choice of difficulties and less means of extricating himself than I have. However, under a full persuasion of the justice of our cause, I cannot but think the prospect will brighten, although for wise purposes it is at present hidden under a cloud." Well, he was ultimately right. Five years later, Washington—and we acknowledge his French allies—surrounded the British army at Yorktown. Cornwallis surrendered, the Revolution prevailed, and a nation was born. We have much more to do over the next two, the next six, the next 20 years. Great things that last are built in decades, not election cycles. There will be more victories and there will be more defeats. There will be more triumphs and there will be more disappointments. This is the nature of all undertakings of this weight and of this magnitude. There are no great men or great causes in the pages of history that did not suffer the same. But we are persuaded of the justice of our cause and we too will triumph in the end. And though we are prepared for the twists and turns and the ups and downs of the road ahead, of this I believe with all my heart: that we in this generation will leave for our children and our grandchildren what Americans always leave for their children and their grandchildren, the single greatest nation in the history of the world. I think we sometimes forget that yes, I'm not saying the stock market is irrelevant and unimportant, but for most people, the health of our economy has nothing to do with the stock market. The health of their economy is whether they have enough money to be able to afford the things they need and want. That's how they judge the health of our economy. So we do have a lot of work to make these arguments. People call it populism; I just call it common sense. I think it's important though that it all starts with a founding belief, or the foundation of making these arguments is expressing to people the extraordinary story of America. And one of the things that's most extraordinary about our history is this unbroken chain of each generation leaving the next better off with a safer, better, more prosperous country. And that's the challenge before us now, because I do think there's a legitimate fear that we will be the first generation to leave the next worse off. And that can't happen. That's un-American. It runs against our story. It runs against our history. And I think the foundation for all of it is explaining to people that America is exceptional, but it's not self-perpetuating. It has always required those in charge to do what it takes to keep it moving forward, and that that's what we're called to do, and then have confidence that we can do it. It's an enormous undertaking, and it's, like I said in my speech, it's something that happens over years and decades, not election cycle to election cycle. Hi. Thank you for being here. The AI Debate is No Longer Boom vs Bust: Why It’s Time to Go Back to Stock Picking | The Weekly Wrap

Steve Eisman(243.0K)·27:07·256.1K views·2026-11-13
Steve EismanHost, The Weekly Wrap Jensen HuangFormer CEO, NVIDIA
TL;DRSteve Eisman argues that the AI trade requires a differential diagnosis with distinct winners and losers following the midterms, Jensen Huang’s resignation, and data center moratorium threats, while the housing market stagnates at a 16-year low.
Shift in Focus: The AI narrative has evolved from capex and neocloud debt concerns to political threats following a Democratic sweep of Congress. Data Center Moratoriums: Many elections were won on platforms opposing data centers, raising fears that construction will be...

TL;DR

Steve Eisman argues that the AI trade requires a differential diagnosis with distinct winners and losers following the midterms, Jensen Huang’s resignation, and data center moratorium threats, while the housing market stagnates at a 16-year low.

AI Market Dynamics & Narrative Shifts

  • Shift in Focus: The AI narrative has evolved from capex and neocloud debt concerns to political threats following a Democratic sweep of Congress.
  • Data Center Moratoriums:
    • Many elections were won on platforms opposing data centers, raising fears that construction will be curtailed or halted nationwide.
    • Eisman notes the irony of tech executives publicly warning that AI is dangerous enough to destroy mankind right before a general election.
  • Market Impact: The combination of elections, core debt, wobbly Anthropic IPO valuations, and the Jensen Wong drama have sucked trillions of dollars in valuation from the stock market and retirement accounts.
  • The Bull Case (Slower Capex): A 10% to 30% slowdown in AI data center construction could cause AI capex to peak in 2027 and hyperscalers to return to positive cash flow by as early as the second half of 2027.
  • Treasury Market Relief: Slower capex would ease the crowding-out effect on US Treasuries caused by $500 billion in AI debt needs expected in 2026, putting downward pressure on long-term interest rates.
  • Local Permitting Strategy: Cash payments from tech firms to local communities (improving schools, lowering taxes) may successfully overcome local political resistance once spotlights turn down.

Negative Housing Data & Cost of Living

  • Stagnant Market: Existing home sales decreased 5.5% to an annualized rate of 3.75 million, hitting a 16-year low.
  • The Lock-In Effect: Most homeowners refinanced into 3% to 3.5% mortgages during COVID; buying a new home at 7% mortgage rates makes selling economically unviable.
  • Inventory vs. New Listings:
    • Active inventory in October hit 183,500 homes in the US, up 7.4% year-over-year (the fastest annual growth rate in almost a year).
    • New listings are falling simultaneously, down 7.5% year-over-year, indicating buyers are refusing to buy at current high prices and sellers are keeping listings private.
  • Escalating Ownership Costs: Property taxes, property and casualty insurance, permits, and renovation costs are estimated to be at least 50% higher than five years ago.

Earnings & Company Performance

  • Charter:
    • Steve Eisman admitted he was wrong, threw in the towel, and sold his shares after two bad quarters.
    • Revenue was down 1.5%, adjusted EBITDA was down 4%, and broadband customers dropped by a whopping 233,000; the stock is down 44% this year.
  • Meritage (Housing): With the housing market severely depressed, Eisman threw in the towel on this stock as well.
  • Skydance (Paramount & Warner Bros. combination):
    • Q3 revenue reached $7 billion, up 5% year-over-year.
    • Operating income was a meager $368 million (5% margin), and net income was just $73 million, weighed down by unprofitable legacy media.
  • CoreWeave:
    • Went public on March 27, 2025 at $39; stock trades at $56 after peaking at $138 in May 2025.
    • The three founders have combined to sell over $3 billion in stock since going public, while buying back zero shares.
    • Nvidia has invested $2 billion in CoreWeave, creating a safety net perception that may weaken under new leadership following Jensen Huang's resignation.
    • Q3 revenue reached $3.7 billion, up 168%, but EPS was a large negative $1.27.
    • Operating cash flow was positive $2 billion against $9.8 billion in investing cash flows (equipment purchases), resulting in a negative free cash flow of $7.8 billion.
    • Total long-term debt and operating lease liabilities hit $54 billion, with debt currently priced at a prohibitive 15%.
  • Nebius:
    • Q3 loss was 13 cents (improved from a loss of 40 cents last year); revenue reached $981 million, up over 500%.
    • FY 2026 revenue is expected at the low end of the $3 billion to $3.4 billion range (consensus was $3.35 billion); 2027 revenue is guided to $12 billion to $13 billion.
    • Capex guidance shocked investors: FY 2026 capex raised to $27 billion (from $20B–$25B), and FY 2027 capex projected at $40 billion to $45 billion (well above expectations of $29 billion), causing the stock to fall 19%.
  • Cisco:
    • Reported Q1 fiscal 2027 results (fiscal year ends July 31st).
    • EPS of $1.40 was up 40% year-over-year (a beat); revenue of $18.5 billion was up 24% year-over-year (a beat).
    • Raised EPS and fiscal revenue guidance for fiscal 2027, demonstrating strong current picks-and-shovels demand.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Gary Gensler / CFTC / SEC Prediction (via guest interview preview): Gary Gensler publicly stated that AI infrastructure is being built before sufficient revenues exist to support it, leading to a correction within the next 18 to 30 months.
  • Steve Eisman Housing Prediction: Melody Wright of Unicus Research predicts housing prices will decline all over the country over the next few years, a trajectory Eisman believes will happen if mortgage rates and ownership costs do not improve.
  • Steve Eisman AI Capex Prediction: AI data center construction projects will slow down by approximately 10% to 30%, which will cause AI capex to peak in 2027 and hyperscalers to return to positive cash flow by as early as the second half of 2027.

Notable Quotes

"The election combined with the coreweave debt, the wobbly foundations of the anthropic IPO valuation, and now the Jensen drama have all sucked trillions of dollars in valuation from the stock market and voters retirement accounts." — Steve Eisman
"It might be just me, but publicly asserting that your technology is so dangerous it can destroy mankind and saying that on the eve of a general election strikes me as amongst the strangest behavior I have ever seen in my entire investing life." — Steve Eisman
"Most fantasy novels are about the hero's journey. Robin Hobb wrote a story about a hero's life." — Steve Eisman
Now, it's going to take a while to figure out the full implications of the resignation of Jensen Wong. We know the election combined with the coreweave debt, the wobbly foundations of the anthropic IPO valuation, and now the Jensen drama have all sucked trillions of dollars in valuation from the stock market and voters retirement accounts. There was some extremely negative news about housing that came out this week. Inventory is increasing, but buyers aren't buying. That is the lowest rate in 16 years. But it's actually worse than that. Cisco reported Thursday night, "These are very good numbers for Cisco." Charter reported last Friday and the results were not good. In last week's rap, I pointed out that there could be a silver lining in the roll back. If, and I want to emphasize it's a big if, AI data center construction slows by, let's say, something like 10 to 30%, then we can see some really positive implications. First, it would mean that AI capex would peak in 2027 and hyperscalers would therefore return to positive cash flow by perhaps as soon as the second half of 2027. In my view, that would be a big positive as investors have grown sick of all the debt and equity raised by the hyperscalers. The less dependent the AI story is on the fixed income markets, in my view, the better. I'd also point out that the Neoclouds would still be in trouble because they are still dependent on the fixed income markets for all their growth. It would also mean that the growth rates of the picks and shovel companies which are the equipment and power suppliers to AI data centers would slow. This is a long list of companies that would include Dell, Cisco, Arista, Eaton, GE, Vernova and Quanta and many others. Yes, the growth rates would slow, but the cycle might be elongated. On the further positive side, slower capex spending means less crowding out effects on US treasuries. As I have discussed before, US Treasury yields remain stubbornly high and a large part of that is because treasuries compete with AI in selling debt. The US government is to some degree subject to the same economic supply and demand patterns as corporate debt issuers. Hence the stubborn yield on treasuries. Just as a quick recap, the US government has to fund its deficit by borrowing, which means selling US treasuries. A crowding out effect means that there is a borrower in the same marketplace chasing the same funding dollars. AI debt needs, which are 500 billion in 2026, are so large that they are having a crowding out effect on the Treasury market. It's putting pressure on long-term interest rates, which in turn puts pressure on the US government deficit. Less AI capex might mean an easing up on interest rates, which would be good for everyone. A Substack subscriber pointed out that as data center construction projects start to get cancelled or placed into review, the market will react poorly because it won't know how far this will go. a very valid point, but I think in a few months we will have a much better idea of how much construction will slow. Again, my guess is that a slowdown of 10% to 30% of projects is where we are headed and that will be okay. "Xia" | A 6-Hour Interview with Soulframe Liang, Producer of Phantom Blade Zero

Zhang Xiaojun Podcast(44.0K)·387:28·2.9K views·2026-11-13
Xiaojun ZhangHost, Zhang Xiaojun Podcast Soulframe LiangProducer, Phantom Blade Zero
TL;DRSoulframe Liang, producer of Phantom Blade Zero, discusses his 15-year journey from an indie developer to leading S-GAME, the deep influence of Gu Long's dark wuxia aesthetics on his work, and the evolution of the xia spirit into modern individual self-actualization.
*The True Nature of Xia: Liang Qiwei explains that a xia differs from an ordinary good person by operating under extreme conditions, bearing massive risks, and maintaining human compassion amidst systemic injustice and collapsing order. - Grassroots Roots: Liang notes that...

TL;DR

Soulframe Liang, producer of *Phantom Blade Zero*, discusses his 15-year journey from an indie developer to leading S-GAME, the deep influence of Gu Long's dark *wuxia* aesthetics on his work, and the evolution of the *xia* spirit into modern individual self-actualization.

1. Defining the *Xia* and Dark *Wuxia* Aesthetics

  • The True Nature of *Xia*: Liang Qiwei explains that a *xia* differs from an ordinary good person by operating under extreme conditions, bearing massive risks, and maintaining human compassion amidst systemic injustice and collapsing order.
  • Grassroots Roots: Liang notes that unlike Western knights who protected feudal lords or Japanese samurai serving shoguns, Chinese *xia* emerge from the margins (*jianghu*) in opposition to the imperial court.
  • The "Pathological" Aesthetic: Drawing from Gu Long's works (e.g., *Sentimental Swordsman, Ruthless Sword*), Liang highlights that *wuxia* characters invariably carry flaws or "pathologies" (e.g., Li Xunhuan’s tuberculosis, Fu Hongxue's lameness), creating an aesthetic collision between extreme beauty and extreme darkness.

2. Evolution of *Wuxia* and Modern Values

  • Shift from Self-Sacrifice to Self-Actualization: Liang contrasts classic *wuxia* tropes of self-abnegation for another person with modern values where young people prioritize personal growth, inner peace, and the "power fantasy" of leveling up (*"In hardship, cultivate yourself; in success, better the world"*).
  • The "Wuxia Punk" Paradigm: Liang argues that traditional *wuxia* moves from the self outward to others, whereas modern "wuxia punk" moves from the self back to the self—mirroring the closed-loop growth found in modern web novels and Soulslike games.
  • Rejection of Preachy Tropes: Liang admits early trailers for *Phantom Blade Zero* failed when they pasted on grand, hypocritical slogans like *"The greatest xia serves the nation and the people,"* which players immediately saw through as insincere "self-gilding."

3. Liang Qiwei's Entrepreneurial Journey and S-GAME

  • The 2011 Startup Pivot: After failing an engineering economics course and having his Tsinghua grad school recommendation revoked, Liang studied architecture at Yale before returning to China in May 2011 with a backpack and a dream to make games.
  • Early Angel Funding: Xu Xiaoping (ZhenFund) personally wired Liang 1,000,000 RMB without a term sheet to start S-GAME in Beijing's Huaqing Jiayuan.
  • The Trap of Capitalization (2015–2017): Liang reflects on the era following *Phantom Blade 1*'s commercial success (pulling in 10 to 20 million RMB monthly with a 20-person team), where he and his team drifted into roadshows, business meetings, and deal-making, losing their core *jianghu* edge.
  • Tencent Investment and Self-Publishing: Following mobile titles like *Phantom Blade 2* and *3* (which peaked at 150 million RMB in monthly revenue), S-GAME bought back their shares from investors, secured over 400 million RMB from Tencent for a ~20% stake, and ultimately chose to self-publish *Phantom Blade Zero* to maintain complete creative control.

4. Game Design, AI, and Industry Perspectives

  • Craft vs. Dao: Liang humbly describes his team's work as possessing solid technical craft (*shu*), while acknowledging their inner cultivation (*dao*) still falls short of legendary industry creators.
  • The 99% Internal Rationale: Liang asserts that external hot trends (like esports or anime styles in *Phantom Blade 2*) account for only 1% of success; 99% relies on the creator's internal rationale, lived experience, and genuine personality.
  • The Limits of AI in Single-Player Games: While pro-AI for technical optimization, Liang emphasizes that AI cannot replace the brand premium and consumer trust built by human labor, painstaking artistry, and auteur-driven creation over decades.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Liang Qiwei predicts that the era of relying on external industry tailwinds or quick market pivots is over for Chinese AAA games, forcing developers to rely entirely on authentic, original IP and deep internal cultivation.

Notable Quotes

*"Only in darkness can a meteor's light illuminate eternity."* — Liang Qiwei (quoting Gu Long)
*"We know exactly what you are... It's hatred, vengeance, being wronged... So why are you suddenly serving me some bleeding-heart saint act?"* — Liang Qiwei (translating core player reactions to insincere narrative framing)
*"The pursuit itself is the goal... You've attained advanced martial arts; what you pursue is even more advanced martial arts. There is no other purpose."* — Liang Qiwei
Your colleague told me the sixteen characters that sum up Phantom Blade Zero: "Framed for slaying his master, a fugitive at the ends of the earth, walking alone into the labyrinth, love and hate intertwined." Maybe we should start today's conversation from there. First—why those sixteen characters? Perhaps they're a modern distillation of the wuxia motif. I think hatred and injustice are the main driving force of all wuxia works. That is also what the wuxia motif— what distinguishes it from other literary genres. So hatred and injustice are actually the cause, and the chivalric spirit of xia is the fruit. First, this world of yours has to hold a vast conspiracy. There has to be a powerful driving force. And no driving force compares to hatred and injustice. That is the xia— one who holds fast in the most extreme form. Right in the midst of that immense hatred and injustice, holding fast to one's true heart in a way beyond ordinary people— anyone like that, I think, can be called a xia. But the conduct that finally emerges can take many forms—going to save others, say, or refusing to be blinded in the face of hatred, or when some misunderstanding arises, when injustice strikes— how he carries himself through it. Those are the outward forms of different stories. What do you think separates a xia from what we'd ordinarily call a good person? And xia come in ranks, too— some are xia, and some are great xia. What's the difference between them? I don't know if this is the right way to explain it. The "goodness" of a xia is first of all something achieved through extraordinary willpower and extraordinary means of resolution. It's a goodness that takes nothing less. It can't be ordinary goodness— say, helping an old lady across the street, or returning a lost wallet. That makes you a good person, a good person in everyday life, but it doesn't require extraordinary willpower or ability. Whereas a xia, I think, with extraordinary resolve and ability, overcomes immense hardship and accomplishes what everyone believes you should—keeping your humanity, or doing what is right. Take the hatred and injustice we just mentioned— under that vast shroud, being able to keep thinking with human compassion— only that can be called xia. For instance, in modern life, if you help an old lady across the street, that doesn't count as xia. But— say the old lady is in the middle of the road and faints, with traffic surging past on either side. You step in and help her up, and you're not afraid of others accusing you, saying, "If you didn't knock her down, why would you help her?" But you still shoulder that risk and take the old lady lying in the middle of the road to a place of safety. That act, I'd say, is xia. So it requires relatively extreme conditions—it needs an environment of extremes, and relatively great risk. And it requires you to give something— whether of mind, of determination, or of conviction—it demands something of you. Must a xia necessarily sacrifice himself? If you're not exposed to enormous risk, you only count as a good person. But a xia must always bear enormous risk. Take, for example, a Guo Jing or a Qiao Feng— or there are also some whose personal spirit, whose sense of self, is stronger—like Yang Guo or Linghu Chong. Each bore enormous risk in their own way. And why do the Chinese pin their vision of so-called justice on figures at society's margins? He holds no real power of law enforcement. He has no law-enforcement power— why does he carry this imagined legitimacy? In that kind of worldview, order has already collapsed. Normal order can no longer be upheld by law or by the authorities. That is the soil from which xia are able to spring forth. It is, first, the soil in which hatred and injustice can breed, and at the same time the soil where such radiant xia can appear. As Gu Long said: only in darkness can a meteor's light illuminate eternity. And the characters—why always this identity of the outsider? You also said before that they're a kind of grassroots hero. I'd approach it from two angles. The most essential trait of the xia is that they are indeed society's outsiders, or at least of the grassroots class. It's hard to call an imperial palace master the protagonist of a wuxia drama. If it is an imperial palace master, he'd at least be one who has defected, or one being hunted down. Or something like Brotherhood of Blades, or the like— an official law enforcer who in the end becomes an outsider. Generally speaking, in the old wuxia novels, things like the Eastern Depot, or the Jinyiwei — they're all villains. Right. So because the core of our wuxia and, we might say, that of other cultures— like Japan's samurai class or the West's knights— is in fact fundamentally different. And where does the difference lie? Our xia comes bound up with the word jianghu, and jianghu itself stands opposed to the imperial court. Jianghu is jianghu— it's the peddlers and porters, the common folk, the nine schools and three teachings, people of all trades and walks of life, the extraordinary heroes who spring up from among the common people, figures on the margins. So it carries a strong grassroots and social character. But if we look at Japan's samurai—what are they? They serve the shoguns and the clans, in service to them—loyalty is their most essential virtue. And the Western knight— where does his glory come from? From guarding the estates of feudal lords. His so-called code of chivalry was, first of all, about these nobles— to defend the interests of the aristocracy they needed certain qualities, certain virtues. That's the Western knight. But the Chinese xia, across all those long feudal centuries, emerged in the crevices of society, because Chinese society was simply too vast. The outsiders and grassroots folk who rose up in the jianghu— the xia who appeared among them. First and foremost, their grassroots nature had to be manifest. Because this touches on something I've observed—this may be my own formulation but I believe it has appeared in many works before. The xia has another very important trait—he's not only marginal. It has already formed a fully complete aesthetic system. It absolutely has an aesthetic system. Absolutely a very complete aesthetic system. And this aesthetic system is, in essence, also extreme. We often say that the aesthetic system of wuxia. How should I put its extremity? It is the collision between extreme beauty and extreme darkness. When I was a child I don't know if you newer players have ever seen New Dragon Gate Inn. I have. New Dragon Gate Inn. Its desert scenery is stunning. Right. That desert scenery even established what we now picture in our minds the wuxia aesthetic in the Chinese imagination. Tony Leung Ka-fai as Zhou Huai'an, riding a camel. That is beautiful against a vast, magnificent landscape. But what about the scenes inside that inn? And what was happening down in the inn's basement? In the final showdown with Eunuch Cao, played by Donnie Yen, that scene genuinely horrified me when suddenly a hand was stripped clean to white bone. Right. Truly chilling. It cast a deep shadow over my young mind back then. I still remember it to this day. I believe that, in essence, this is the aesthetics of wuxia. That is wuxia aesthetics. Today's players and readers, for instance, often idolize works like Japan's Berserk. They love Berserk, they love the darkness expressed in Soulslike games, the utter darkness and desolate vistas, an aesthetic of exquisitely beautiful, desolate scenery. But in fact, if you look at the old wuxia films, in our wuxia films, or the visual expression of wuxia themes, perhaps half a century ago, they were already exploring this very type of aesthetic. Because only by taking your psyche, your aesthetic sense, and swinging it back and forth across an enormous spectrum, exploring a vast range, can you bring about that ultimate sense of impact. That's what makes wuxia so appealing, isn't it? That's why it draws people in. Because it offers a genre like this one that lets you make such enormous leaps within it. So when we speak of wuxia it's precisely not about its noble, glorious, righteous side. It's precisely because of its many dark facets, its extremes, that something is born of that extremity. It is a form of opposition. Only with darkness can you bring out the light. Without darkness there is no light. And without, say, the buildup you cannot deliver that shock at the crucial moment, no matter how beautiful, no matter how good that stunning power may be. Do you think wuxia, viewed across its history, has undergone major changes? From Jin Yong's worldview, including Jin Yong's worldview which itself also changed right? Yes—then Gu Long carried it forward. What direction did that change take? And then to our modern... to the modern web novels or modern wuxia games and xianxia. Or xianxia, yes. I believe this evolution certainly happened—it must be tied to the social conditions of each era and to people's aesthetic preferences. These things are all connected. In ancient times, under the feudal dynasties, what it depicted was social reality. From The Seven Heroes and Five Gallants to something like Water Margin, or even the wuxia of the Han dynasty, much earlier still. The xia are those who defy the law's prohibitions through force of arms. That lineage carried all the way into the modern era and on to the present day. Speaking of the modern age, especially after the New Wuxia movement, the center of gravity of wuxia creativity shifted to Hong Kong. And what kind of place is Hong Kong? At the time, when wuxia was being written there, it was a colonial culture. And a colonial culture was bound to absorb and fuse the clashing influences of Eastern and Western cultures and to create something new. For instance, Jin Yong, very typically, drew on techniques of characterization from Western classics to shape his wuxia novels. He married the classic Western novel with our traditional Chinese chaptered novels. It was a hybrid. On the one hand it felt steeped in old-world flavor yet at the same time its readability, its way of building characters, was actually thoroughly Western. With Gu Long, this went even further. Gu Long had already fused everything from Japanese chanbara films to Western detective novels and, later on, the modern screenwriting theory he developed from them. He wrote with brevity and punch, and kept his paragraphs extremely short. He also incorporated the serialized format making it an even more exhilarating read. Right. So the earliest shoots of that were already sprouting. And with Huang Yi it had perhaps already evolved into something quite close to the system of modern web novels. So wuxia has always moved in step with the development of each society and the character of its times. So what has happened to it today? I feel that over the past few decades it has actually been stuck in a gap where the new has not succeeded the old. And what's behind that? Colonial culture is fading away. Hong Kong no longer has that colonial culture, you see. It belonged to a crevice between eras of history, to that fissure in history. So wuxia, born as it was of colonial culture, was inevitably going to be replaced or else had to return to its roots—returning to our broader, more unadulterated tradition — one that is Chinese at its core. But then, what exactly is the new? Nothing has emerged. In other words, over the past twenty years this thing has not really developed at all. But it has spawned other variants. I see this as a shift in society's overall values — perhaps even a sign of progress. People focus more on the self. "Am I living well?" "Am I happy?" "How I am doing matters most." I can delight in helping others. I can help people and what helping others brings me is my own inner peace. Even when I act with the chivalric spirit of xia, it's for myself to gain inner peace. That's fine. But I can't endlessly — as if I had no drive of my own, existing purely for another person. I think that's something our modern value system cannot accept. It's not sacrifice. It's choice. It is a choice. And as for myself, certainly — what lies at my very core? At my core, I myself must become better. I must become a better person, whether that's growing stronger in ability, or grasping some profounder truth. It makes readers feel good, and rightly so — because getting stronger itself chimes with our current social mood. I must grow stronger.
