What kind of conspiracy structure gives it greater dramatic conflict? I think that's true. You can write a conspiracy with great complexity. You could even make it purely cerebral, mind-bending stuff. But in the end I found that every conspiracy, every structure ultimately comes back to one essential thing — each person's motive. And those motives naturally give shape to that larger framework. As a science student, an architecture student at that, I'm extremely sensitive to this. Like, if I read any other novel after I finish it, I'll roughly form a framework of it in my mind. A framework of how the plot unfolds. What happens from this point to that point. Whether those threads actually connect. And I've found that in many novels those threads don't connect — they're wrong. In the real world, things wouldn't happen that way. Because the lines of logic, the framework itself, are off. So I've spent a lot of time studying this. In fact, I've recently thought about using AI to build one. You give it a novel and it maps out a structural diagram. That's something AI can do very easily. I consider this a form of aesthetics, really. The structure itself is an aesthetic. It's fascinating. It just so happens that this pursuit engages both sides of the brain at once. But the weakness is you get trapped in the clever concept, lost in the idea, sucked into it. And once you're lost in the idea, what it leads to is this: Your characterization, your portrayal of emotion and character is either lacking, or you borrow classic archetypal figures and just fill them in. Good to have. Maybe that's just something that can't be helped. Because the main energy really goes into building the structure. So there were times when your characters were filled in from other works. A lot. Honestly, the moment you create characters you're bound to follow certain archetypes. I don't think that's anything that needs hiding. So you'd call this your weak spot? Possibly. It might be a shortcoming. But I think, as a driving force behind structure and plot progression, and the feeling it evokes as it unfolds, I think this is still quite an interesting thing, especially stories where the character bears a past—your story takes place in the middle of the real, full story, and with every step forward you take, new events converge with the old backstory. That kind of story is really quite interesting. Like, for example, take John Wick. John Wick, essentially, has a bit of that flavor. Every character he encounters in his storyline is, in his original story, not some newly invented figure—they're all people from his past being brought before the audience again and again. It's just that now his old colleagues have come to hunt him down. That kind of story—I find it really interesting. Even though the structure is simple, it has this sense of ripples spreading through real life. Like a kind of butterfly effect. But this is also a device wuxia novels use all the time—the moment something like this appears, you immediately feel it's deeply wuxia. Like, "Eighteen years ago, such-and-such happened." "At the battle of Yanmen Pass, we lost so many of our own." That atmosphere—you see, the moment lines like these appear, you're instantly plunged into a wuxia world. "In the final years of such-and-such dynasty, in the twilight of the dynasty, chaos and war everywhere." And then, as he's speaking, something else comes up about eighteen years ago. It's just so wuxia, isn't it? As I've mentioned before, I wrote an article on Zhihu arguing that Blossoms is a wuxia drama. Spot on. Right. A friend of mine forwarded that article to Wong Kar-wai, and Director Wong read it. Director Wong said, "He got it right." But where did I feel that strongest wuxia sensibility came from at the time? There's a small subplot in it—how, years ago, Mr. A was wiped out in some bloodbath, how there was some kind of plan, back in the early days when China's stock market had just begun, Mr. A led a group of people to go do such-and-such, and in the end they became the crew left collecting the bodies. A plot like that—I mean, that is very wuxia. That is completely a wuxia plotline. Except the martial arts are swapped for the financial market. Right. Yes. It's that something which happened maybe ten, twenty years ago has shaped the world of today, forging an extreme personality. In Blossoms, what he depicts with Mr. A and Li Li—that storyline—is one of extreme personality, a personality born of some affair years ago. And that is a very wuxia narrative structure. So have you ever thought about what exactly makes wuxia so captivating to you? Because the entire worldview of your game is built around it—very few people do that, as a game developer. Honestly, I don't think I've wholesale absorbed the things of wuxia. What I've taken from wuxia is what I believe still has vitality, what can serve the new entertainment products we'll create in the future—that material—connecting it with a modern, new mode of creation. I think that's really how I approach it. I'm not someone who rigidly follows, say, traditional wuxia, or even the new-school wuxia novels and wuxia films—I don't strictly adhere to that whole system. In high school you read more Gu Long than Jin Yong. Is that so? Did you feel their worldviews were different back then? My entry point to Gu Long in high school was Jiao Enjun's Little Li Flying Dagger. It was premiering at the time. It's since become a classic, but back then it was a first run. I was completely hooked on the TV series, so I started reading—picked up the original novel, Sentimental Swordsman, Heartless Sword. After reading it, I felt it was somewhat impenetrable. I couldn't understand it. First of all, the prose is hard to chew. The style itself feels forbidding. Reading it—for someone who's never encountered it before, coming to it cold, it's a bit like someone who's never drunk alcohol taking their first sip. You'd definitely feel—you wouldn't think alcohol tastes good at all. You drink Moutai or whatever and it tastes awful—assuming you're not a drinker. If you drink, you won't think it's anything special. You'll just find it bitter. But once you're drawn in, or when you return to it later, you realize that's exactly the flavor you want. That's precisely the taste you're after. This—it's a bit obscure, he doesn't speak plainly. Doesn't speak plainly. What they say is rather odd—the dialogue itself is strange. You can't even imagine how bizarre those lines would sound if voiced aloud. That's the kind of feeling it gives. But when you read it, that's exactly the flavor you want. Without that flavor, I wouldn't bother reading it at all. And later on, too—Gu Long had works ghostwritten by his disciples, and one glance tells you the flavor isn't there. So at first, I actually didn't like it. The heavy reading came later, in college—that's when there was a lot more Gu Long. So the first Gu Long you encountered was also Little Li Flying Dagger. Right, exactly. Sentimental Swordsman, Heartless Sword. Reading Li Xunhuan as a kid versus reading him now—the frame of mind is completely different. When I first read it, the kick was too strong. You couldn't even register things like the cuckoldry, why someone made those sacrifices—none of that registered yet. What grips you first is the structure. The way it moves forward is so strange — why is it here one moment, there the next? If it were a camera, his camera is constantly cutting, constantly montaging. But later, Gu Long — for instance, when he wrote The Eleventh Son — he, by his very nature, wrote it using screenplay techniques. But once you've grown used to other, easier-to-digest novels and you suddenly pick up Gu Long, his style of writing is like nothing you've ever seen. And I think writers who came after would find it very hard to imitate that style of writing, because in his writing, his camera never stops switching. Interesting. So where do you think that edge of Gu Long's came from? Because his own life was full of upheaval. His life is something you can't replicate, so his works can't be replicated either. In the end, it always comes down to the person's own life. A young street gangster — he essentially lived his own life into someone just like his protagonists. And he had no family. Even though he had plenty — he had children, he had been married, but he never had a traditional family. Before he died, he actually asked, "Where are all my girlfriends?" "Why hasn't a single one of them come?" He drank like a madman. He drank with the gangsters of Taiwan and Hong Kong back in those days. Because he owed money, the gangsters were going to chop off one of his fingers, so he chugged that entire bottle of XO or whatever it was. A man like that — we have no way of ever getting close to him, or of reaching that kind of life he lived. We can't even imagine how he carried on that way. And so, it's no surprise he could write novels like those. Do you envy it — Gu Long's life? I wouldn't envy it, but I do admire him for it. How could he, in that era — how was he even able to live a life like that? That, too, is a kind of aesthetics — a "morbid" aesthetic. That, too, is a kind of "sickness." His own life was quite "morbid," quite strange. But isn't that also a kind of aesthetics? Many people are like this. Many people — an author whose life is an utterly bland one — the works he writes are destined never to be works you'd find interesting. Gu Long is a textbook case — he lived his own life into a puddle of mud. Not one of us would want to live the way he did. And that's why he died so young, and the cause of his early death was plain: it was precisely the excessive drinking that led to this. There was one time when he got slashed by someone, and the blood transfusion carried some — you know — liver disease. In the end, it went straight to his liver, and then he kept drinking on top of it. His life had already become that same puddle. Now, seen from an outsider's perspective, that looks rather beautiful, but you certainly wouldn't want it yourself, right? You yourself wouldn't want to be gone in your forties, after all. So perhaps he's just — but when we set out to appreciate him, we feel that he himself is a work of art. His life is a work of art, and the works he wrote out of that life-as-art have been preserved. And that may be why so many people love him fanatically. Right — his works are even more "morbid" still. Yes, even more fractured. Yes — a lot of people say, for instance, that Jin Yong's works, given 100 people, will win over about 70% of them. Gu Long's works, among those same 100 people, can only attract 30. But those 30 love him fanatically — those 30 all love him one hundred percent. Characters like Chu Liuxiang, Li Xunhuan, Fu Hongxue, Hua Manlou — who was your favorite as a kid? Did you ever fantasize about becoming one of them? Well, if you're a normal person — if you're a normal person in modern society — you wouldn't want to become any of Gu Long's protagonists. Because they're not happy. They live in deep pain. You can only admire them. They're exactly like the life of Gu Long's I just described: displayed there as a work of art, and you admire it. You admire the Venus de Milo — it's all the more beautiful precisely because its arms are gone. But do you want your own arms broken off? Of course not. We're all ordinary people. And ordinary people can only live our ordinary lives right where we are, while imagining — wow, the kind of chaotic lives some people lead — as he's put in a display case and exhibited as a work, and you admire him. So appreciation comes from an outsider's perspective. From the perspective of the other. If it happened to me, I wouldn't want to become that at all. And then, of course, there's Li Xunhuan — you know, that — Fu Hongxue is cool too, so dark. You're also admiring a certain aesthetics in his story structures. The structures of his stories are extraordinarily rich, and they leap around tremendously. They leap around intensely. Extremely jumpy — if you analyze them with classical literary theory, every rule gets violated. Every single one. As for Gu Long's novels — if you judge them by — well, Jin Yong's or Liang Yusheng's novels are rigorously disciplined, their structures immaculate. They feel thoroughly upright, thoroughly proper, impeccably structured. Gu Long's novels, on the other hand — and by his later novels, Gu Long had already gone completely wild, past all reining in. But those are the better ones — those are the ones I prefer. It's a stronger liquor. So strong most people can't even get it down. But if you're a seasoned connoisseur you find it even more beautiful. Take Chu Liuxiang, for example. Of course, the classics are those earlier volumes like Fragrance in the Sea of Blood or The Thrush or The Great Desert. Those were already brilliant. Gu Long's—you could call them a showcase of his craft. But—he wrote Chu Liuxiang all the way to the final volume Midnight Orchid. Midnight Orchid is an extremely—an extremely short yet razor-sharp work. I'd suggest you go read Midnight Orchid. You can finish it within a single flight. You'll find the cutting is already a stream-of-consciousness style of camera work. For instance, he writes a great battle at one point and the way that battle is depicted—it's extraordinary. I admire it so much I can practically see the storyboards in it and those storyboards cut fast. He writes about this one moment, then jumps to that, the character shifts—wait, how did it become this character now? If you don't read closely, you can't keep up with the thought. Just think—reading text, and you actually can't keep up with the flow of his prose, what kind of experience is that? So I strongly recommend Midnight Orchid if you're a seasoned connoisseur, if you're someone with—a deep reading background, looking for a more challenging reading experience. I think Midnight Orchid is an excellent choice. It suddenly occurs to me—does your thinking happen through visual language or through language—written language, or literary language, right? I think it's all of them. Really, they're blended together. Blended together. Take Gu Long's novels again—they come with storyboards built in; to some extent that's visual language. And in this respect, you'll find that a lot of Japanese anime, especially manga, also have some truly brilliant expressions. It—because manga is a form of expression that sits between pure audiovisual language and pure written language. And you'll notice that in some manga storyboards, both of these things exist at once. It's not pure visuals I care about, nor pure literature—what I love is something that emerges from combining the two, a feeling of endless aftertaste you can't experience in everyday life. There's so much of that in manga. Let me just give a random example, like, say, near the end of Slam Dunk, all those pages of silent panels without a single line of dialogue—that storyboard sequence. In their final game against Sannoh, the last few dozen panels have no dialogue—they're all still. Or take Toguro in Yu Yu Hakusho—Toguro in Yu Yu Hakusho—Toguro is an early villain, a boss. You defeat him, and then he— He'd been wearing sunglasses the whole time. When he parts with Genkai, he walks a couple of steps forward, then suddenly turns back and takes off the sunglasses, revealing the most gentle eyes. Because up to that point, this character was all muscle, a monster of a figure. And at that moment he suddenly turns his head and shows such a tender gaze. Any storyboard moment like that, I think, has earned its place in the history of audiovisual language as a classic moment. So I feel that I—whether I'm reading Gu Long's novels, or these manga, or certain films, what I'm looking at is the same thing. It's hard to say whether it's literary or pictorial or audiovisual—it's a fusion. It's a fusion that creates an authentic feeling, a composite feeling. So why did all that push you toward games rather than manga? What can games do that manga can't? Because in a sense, games circle back to it—they can present that approach even better. Of course, games are also more modern, more commercially viable as a form, with more creative techniques, a richer medium. With games, there's so much more you can do. The way you can pursue that kind of slicing becomes even more complex in games. In games you can push to the absolute extreme of audiovisual spectacle, or you can do—like Disco Elysium, or like many other great indie games— You can go impressionistic, or realist, you can be like a Sony first-party blockbuster, pretty much a Hollywood film, or you can be very subtle and understated, like so many indie games. You can express yourself, or you can combine the two—some parts delivering high production quality, other parts using fragmented storytelling. You can have it all. Take Black Myth: Wukong—within a single game, it weaves together so many kinds of expressive techniques, so many ways of telling a story. Some modern, some traditional, some concrete, some just suggestive—a light touch that says just enough. I think games offer a far broader space and setting for creation. Quite interesting—you said you've always been numb to the real world, yet your senses are razor-sharp toward virtual worlds. Right. That's how it's been since childhood. Why do so many of your creative origins stem from hatred, from schemes? Very dark. You should see what architects wear when they hold a conference, what colors architects wear. Hahaha. Architects wear nothing but black. Hahaha. I think it might come from all those years of architecture training, the aesthetic that training instilled. And that connects to something else. Because architecture, you see, has spent all these years chasing technology, chasing simplicity, that industrial feel. When high-tech architecture — and architects — pursue that, for instance, they very often work in black, white, and gray, black, white, gray — those colors because they want to bring out the essential qualities of the materials themselves. They don't want excessive cladding, soft finishes or colors covering up the character of the building's own materials. So in architecture circles there's a shared aesthetic along those lines. It's—a tendency to reduce everything to just black, white, and gray. I think that may have left its mark on me too. So, back to your middle school days, the kid sitting in the corner who loved comics and novels and still got excellent grades — Liang Qiwei. At that moment, how did you think about the rest of your life? What did you hope for? I didn't think about it at all back then. I think, like the vast majority of Chinese middle schoolers, I don't believe any of us had truly meaningful thoughts about the future. For the vast majority, I think it was really just about doing well on today's test paper, solving today's problems, then doing well on the gaokao and figuring the rest out later. I think that's how most people felt. Of course, some people had their own passions, some loved playing basketball, some loved playing piano. If you got the chance to keep pursuing that passion, you were certainly lucky. But in truth, the vast majority never got that chance. Yet we can't say that all those countless people were unlucky. The vast majority—could you really call the vast majority unlucky? I don't think so. I think that's simply what life is. Life is about living each day well. For a middle schooler that was actually a fairly happy time — even if preparing for the gaokao was grueling, but that grind was only a physical exhaustion. Emotionally, though, I felt certain and steady. The vast majority of us just had to do well on that paper. I wouldn't call that unhappiness. It was a kind of happiness too. Because you had a strong sense of purpose. A strong sense of purpose is itself happiness. You knew what you were doing it all for, what all your striving was for. I just wanted that high score. Right — in middle school everyone's goal was simple. So back then, the mini-boss was the test paper. Exactly. The mini-boss was the test paper. And it was fairly easy to beat. So when you're in a situation like that, you stop believing there's any truth, goodness, or beauty left in the world. You stop believing that if you go all out, if you work hard, you'll succeed. Or you stop believing you have talent, that you have real ability, that you have something worth expressing, that your work of art can be exchanged for genuine success in the real world. That's fascinating. After Phantom Blade 1, you also wrote a letter, a public one—an article. It's quite well written. Let me read you a bit. I don't know if you remember. The title was "Making Money in China, Pure and Simple." Three years earlier, I had taken the one-million-yuan investment from Mr. Xu Xiaoping and sat alone in an empty office, thinking: Where should I even begin? I was completely clueless at the time. I had never worked at a game company. Though I degrees from two elite schools, Tsinghua and Yale, they were both degrees in architecture. It was like a warrior carrying two pieces of orange-quality mage gear in his backpack. The only thing connecting me to games was the two indie games I'd completed alone as a student. The only thing connecting me to games was the two indie games I'd completed alone as a student, the Rainblood series. I did the programming, the script, the art, all myself, completing those two RPGs within four years. Back then I didn't know what an IP was or what "business" meant. Everything was for depicting that virtual world in my mind. To feed that joy, I worked day and night against deadlines and paid a painful price for it. In the eyes of the games industry, those barely counted as games. And in the games industry, I didn't know a single person. The story goes that other founders just pull a whole crew out of their old company or network their way from one contact to the next, with people flocking to answer the call, instantly assembling a team of crack hands. All I could do was recruit a few online friends who had helped me with Rainblood 2: City of Flame. One was a current student; two worked in animation. Uncle H, the animator, spent his days at his company making crude little Flash animations for websites and his nights doing character animations for Rainblood 2 for me. He was quite torn about my offer. He was earning 4,000 yuan a month; I could only offer 2,000. In the end, I gritted my teeth and went up to 3,000. He said fine, but come hell or high water, he would never do simple tween animations again. Even though by 2012 mobile gaming was already a storm about to break, at that time you were still working on Rainblood Prequel: Mirage. At that time you were still working on Rainblood Prequel: Mirage. But a few greenhorns like us knew nothing about the state of the industry. We excitedly prepped for ages and our first project turned out to be a 2D side-scrolling action game for PC, Rainblood Prequel: Mirage. The programmer R you mentioned was in Los Angeles making AAA PlayStation games at a Sony-owned studio, earning over ten thousand US dollars a month. He was coming back to China to take 3,000 yuan. His girlfriend wouldn't have it. They fought over it for a while and broke up. Then, a little over two years later, they were all earning six figures a month, and R found a new girlfriend too. You were clearly quite pleased with yourself at the time. About that state of mind—I think the earlier part of that piece was, honestly, still pretty good looking back at it now, more than a decade later. The latter part, I think, clearly shows I'd gotten carried away. When you start ticking off your real-world successes—which weren't even much of successes—the very fact that you need to show these things off means you have nothing to say—nothing truly remarkable worth holding up in front of people. I think that was extremely typical of me at the time. The experiences from that earlier part just became fodder for bragging in the latter part. I think Phantom Blade 2 is a great product to dissect. Because I don't think it turned out badly at all. Did it actually achieve anything better? Not necessarily. And it didn't. But I think we gained something valuable from it— the lesson being that whether others do this well probably has nothing to do with you. You have to seek within yourself first, before you can seek anything from outside. If you're not cut out for it, then you're simply not the person for this. No matter how good the external conditions, you still won't be the one to do it. And then you— then that market isn't yours. It's a false premise. And what about the reverse? If you truly are the person meant to do this, then however bad the external environment, you can still change that environment. Just like what Game Science later did. So I believe seeking within matters far more than the external environment. Inner exploration matters far more than external objective conditions. Whether that's a buff or a debuff, that's a second-order concern. So with Phantom Blade 2, I feel it basically taught us a lesson. Because there you were, standing on every hot trend— esports, anime-style— whatever could be fused together, we fused. We fused it all. But this, too, is a device of such works— of fantasy—placing a character into extreme circumstances so that we may understand him. There's a "morbid" aesthetic to it as well. It really is quite "morbid," isn't it? Quite "morbid." Have you not seen Soul in our game— what you see most often— clutching at his heart? We've even made Soul "morbid" in body, like a Gu Long male lead. I mean, he— Li Xunhuan always has to cough a few times. He has his own understanding, his own vision, and he carries that vision to its utmost extreme. Yes. If Soul's story celebrates life, urging everyone to cherish it, then what of Zuo Shang? Zuo Shang is about—about calling on everyone to save the world. To save the world. Zuo Shang is the one who truly wants to save the world. Zuo Shang. But Zuo Shang sets out to save the world for his own sake, though his way of saving the world is different. So each of them has their own worldview. It's just that those worldviews collide. In this story, I'm racking my brain for how to put this without spoiling anything. Soul's original intent is to save himself, yet in the end, he saves the world. While Zuo Shang's original intent is to save the world, but in the end, he destroys it. Why is that? Well, that would get into the plot. But anyway, to sum it up, that's how it goes. And that in itself is a kind of worldview. When you believe you're acting for yourself, you may well have done others an immense kindness. That can really happen. When your original intention is simply to live your own life well, to better yourself, the outcome may be that you've contributed to the world and made it a better place. But when your— when your vision is some grandiose crusade to save the world, you may bring disaster down upon it. It may be a kind of delusion. It's rather like — this is something I'd never considered before, like the invaders we used to talk about, or World War II, for that matter. Perhaps it's that those who launch wars may honestly believe they're saving the world. Yet what they actually deliver to the masses of people and to all those nations is disaster. I think that counts as one way of understanding it. That's one way to see it, too. And isn't one of Soul's side quests the mystery of his bloodline? Yes, exactly. Why does a person's origins matter so, so much to the road that follows? It comes down to this: who you are matters. Who you were to begin with still shapes who you're to become. It truly matters. That core is always the growth of the self. You cannot escape it. And I think we Chinese are the people most devoted to this — your bloodline, your heritage. I feel that with Western ideas pouring in nowadays, with all their influence over us, when we bring up the "family of origin" the very first thing that comes to mind is that "family of origin" sounds like a pejorative. We think of fleeing the family of origin, of family-of-origin trauma. The moment your clan lineage comes up, the moment your forebears, generations of them, come up, it feels like something backward, or... how shall I put it. The associations it stirs aren't — they may all be formed under the influence of Western thought. You come to view — things like your family genealogy, your ancestral hall, your elders paying homage at ancestral rites — all things now rather heavily stigmatized. But I believe we Chinese actually must lean on these things to find our own roots. You must find that awakening of the bloodline — where it is you come from, your native soil, your ancestral hall. You have a lineage, a thread. This is bound into the memory of the Chinese people, something that cannot be cast off. And I think it's something profoundly important. With it, we feel settled, grounded. We've forged a connection with the land. I know where I come from — no matter how far I fly, I came, in the end, from that place. I can retreat no further. Take me, for instance — it would be some village in Hainan. My final ancestral hall would trace back to there, and that's as far as it goes for me. No further back. That is my ultimate root. Even though I've been to so many countries — I've traveled far and wide — your ultimate root is still right there. Now, this idea, rendered in romanticized form, means that perhaps a certain power runs in Soul — something in his very blood. In the end, you are destined to awaken that bloodline. That's also why so much of your game is about seeking roots, isn't it? Your martial arts included. Seeking roots. That's its traditional side. The traditional side. Many of your martial arts moves require that you be able to trace them back to their martial-arts foundations. Right, exactly. That's the traditional side, even the side rooted in native soil. Take our lion dance. The lion dance belongs inside the ancestral hall. We once went to a place in Guangdong — for the southern lion dance, I believe. We were there, performing inside that ancestral hall. We put on the lion dance. And we recorded those drums. It was all thoroughly — thoroughly traditional, drawn straight from the very wellspring. And I think, for what we're seeking, this matters a great deal. We as people must find our own roots. Our culture must find its own roots. And the product, in the end, must carry that heritage forward. You must know, in the end, exactly where you came from. You can become supremely cool. However dazzling you become, people can tell at a glance that it's from that root that you grew. It isn't something floating free. However cool we make things, our product reads as the work of people who truly know the craft, who actually went to the wellspring and made it there — that's what this product is. And if what we contrast it with is — say, if you look today at Hollywood films telling ABC stories — Asian-American, Chinese-American stories — you'd recognize that as a kind of interpretation already adrift, floating free of the source. Something imagined, existing merely as fragments of a culture. It may have industrial polish, all manner of technique, but through our eyes as Chinese, when we watch it, we feel its root is missing — because its root was missing from the start. What that is, is a group of — say, ABCs, or ethnic Chinese actors, doing their utmost to gather stray scraps and fragments of their ancestral culture and stitch them together. We truly have roots — we'd never think to patch elements together. We can boldly draw on all kinds of elements, yet everyone can tell at a glance that our foundation is rock solid. Actually, whether it's martial arts or weapons, they're all extensions of a person's character. You can hardly talk about a martial art apart from the personality of the one who practices it. Yes. Right. So with anything we make, what we start from is genuinely very traditional material, and it keeps evolving, evolving, evolving, until finally—yes—something incredibly cool comes out of it. I hear you personally chose all the weapons. What's the thinking behind them? A lot of them I chose myself. Master Yang gives me plenty of advice to draw on— like how a given weapon can be wielded with style. You see, weapons in China—in our traditional culture— are an extension of character. A personified embodiment of cultural elements. Take the Golden Cudgel, the Green Dragon Crescent Blade, the nine-toothed rake— when you say those names, don't the characters immediately come to mind? Each one maps onto a figure— it maps onto that character, onto those stories, onto that personality. So you— you come to feel that it carries a personality, a culture. Could you talk about the design of Soul's and Zuo Shang's weapons? We often speak of the saber and the sword— the sword is the weapon of the scholar-officials. The sword is a ceremonial weapon, resplendent. In ancient times, those who wore swords rarely fought with them in person, because the sword's combat effectiveness in real life— the sword is actually inferior to many more practical weapons, like the spear, or the heavy broadsword. A sword would lose to those in real life. But why do so many wuxia novels love writing about swords? Because the sword is romantic. The sword symbolizes status, an aspiration of the scholar-official class. So in our game there are all kinds of swords, and sword-wielding characters tend to be elegant and ethereal, effortlessly graceful. Of course, Zuo Shang also uses a sword—everyone knows that— and his sword is golden. Forged from pure gold—and gold is a very soft metal. It's simply— in real life it would be useless in combat, because gold is too soft. But in our setting, Zuo Shang has always wielded a sword forged of pure gold. Is that meant to express his flamboyance? A flamboyance, and his absolute self-confidence. Because he— in our world's lore there is also killing intent— he infuses his killing intent into that gold, enabling it to slice through iron like clay. That's the lore of our game. But what it represents is exactly this: his opulence, his absolute confidence and pride. Hey— there's the man you said earlier could never be the protagonist. That very pride. And what about Soul? This collaboration with Donnie Yen (who plays Moyuan and serves as the game's executive producer)— how did it come about? It's really about passing on the tradition. As I mentioned earlier, wuxia as a culture has already entered a bottleneck. And for Donnie Yen, his generation of filmmakers were the very people who created that golden age. That cohort— now some are gradually retiring, or stepping away one way or another. He surely wants this culture to keep being passed down, but carried by a new medium. So when we first reached out, he strongly supported and endorsed the idea. He agreed without hesitation to take on this endeavor together with us. Honestly, I think— to frame it as "lofty sentiment" might come off as mawkish. The truth is, I simply can't put myself into the mindset of Donnie Yen and people like him. I can't speak for him, but— I can venture a guess. He must have hoped that what his generation of filmmakers created — the aesthetics and culture of kung fu cinema that took the world by storm — would not simply be thrown away. He couldn't accept that, on today's new international stage, it should look worse than the chanbara films of the Japanese, or the Americans' gun-fu, or their Westerns. I think for filmmakers of their generation, that would be a very hard reality to accept. Because you have to understand — Hollywood, today's Hollywood action films owe a great deal to that generation of filmmakers. They're even behind Hollywood's current action — action — whether it's the fight performance, the camerawork, or the visuals, all of it bears the influence of that generation of filmmakers. Think of the Hollywood fight scenes we used to watch — how silly they were, how slow. It's so much better now, because so many of our Hong Kong directors, action choreographers, and teams are working over there. Fight scenes as a whole have risen to a whole new level. Look at the fight scenes in Spider-Man now — spectacular. The action performance is excellent, and that too comes from the dedication of many Hong Kong directors working there. So whether out of sentiment, or from having lived through it firsthand, I believe Donnie Yen, toward something like this, must have had certain expectations, must have had hopes. Hopes that probably went beyond joining some project — some commercial collaboration, or any single project — some simple notion like that. It was rather: "After everything it took, the achievements our generation worked so hard for — surely they can't just vanish, can they? They can't just be thrown away. It can't be that twenty years from now, when we talk about action films, action games, they're all Japanese things, or all American things. Surely what we contributed won't just disappear like that." So from that perspective, I believe he definitely had hopes. And a project like ours, well, it fulfilled that hope of his. So as we went on to collaborate, everything went very smoothly and very happily. Has he ever given you any advice? Plenty of advice. Including specific fight moves, and a lot of the dramatic scenes. He'd say a character shouldn't be like that — too old and stodgy. He doesn't want that stodgy old air. He wants something with a more youthful feel. In his own films — his own movies — he always insists on staying cool. He's cool, very sharp — that's his style. And we, too, hope to carry that in the game as much as possible, to recreate that style. For instance, in those Japanese and Western companies, the biggest gainers are the company's owners, the major shareholders. So you think the production company's people are the ones who enjoy the highest returns? And in Japan, it's even more pronounced. But in China, people like us emerged — people who weren't really prepared, or who learn while doing. Rank amateurs, learning as we go, still exploring how games should even be made. Yet because we're at such a favorable stage of market growth, whether through the rise of China's consumer power or Chinese products going global, reaping the benefits of globalization, you find in the end that unprepared people like us have actually enjoyed greater real-world rewards than our Japanese and Western peers. And we're on the modest end — just look at the hugely successful ones. Think about it: which Western producer, or Japanese producer, could ever have as much money as the owner-producers of those successful mobile-game companies? So really, we owe our thanks to the market itself. As for us — as far as we ourselves are concerned, we're just grateful for the chance it gave us to be forged. But we know full well that if you measure things purely by professional standards, there are many shortcomings, many gaps — in theoretical foundations, in creative fundamentals, in technical depth — huge room for improvement in all of it. But with the blessing of this magnificent market of ours, we've been able to work in an immensely vast, very powerful environment like that. So I'd say that counts as an advantage. But we can't let that advantage blind us into believing our abilities are just as strong. In truth, our ability hasn't reached the level of those at the very top. All we're doing is making things as we go, learning as we go, observing as we go, and swiftly catching up, then rapidly overtaking and advancing. America and Japan really follow a more professionalized career track. Only after becoming a world-class producer — like Kojima — do you finally get the chance to start your own company. Just think — at companies overseas, being able to spend over 100 million US dollars to develop a single product is a luxury even many famous producers could never dream of — that kind of condition and opportunity. But then think about China: how many young producers here can put over 100 million dollars into making one game? Even if they're not making a single-player title, they can spend over 100 million on a mobile game or a cross-platform game spanning three platforms. There are actually many companies, many producers, who get to enjoy that kind of resource. That's a fascinating point. We've been talking all morning about how the xia was born of a marginal group, born of the grassroots. And that actually seems to be a Chinese tradition — so much of it sprouts from the bottom up. The way we see it, if you zoom out to the bigger picture, China's industry is itself grassroots in nature. It defies all convention. It doesn't care about your generations of old money or accumulated history. China has no old money anyway — it's all new money. There's no long accumulation of history to speak of, none of those deep-rooted, time-honored sentiments. People just roll up their sleeves and get to work. And I think that's a wonderful thing. It also means that different people within it end up diverging. It offers you the path of the entrepreneur and also the path of staying a creator. But you have to choose for yourselves. You do still keep your identity as an entrepreneur, though. It's just that you're not a particularly good one, because you can't scale up the scope of the business. You can't replicate it or scale it quickly. You've gone off in another direction instead, toward pure creation — creation that simply happens to be under your own control. Do you think that control matters to a creator? I think it matters a great deal. I would actually advise — or rather, strongly advise — all creators who wish to fully express themselves to have thorough control over their own projects. And China happens to provide exactly that soil. China provides precisely that soil. Because we have excellent market conditions, an excellent opportunity. So there really is a chance for you to take the reins of the creation yourself and to secure a certain degree of control over it. And now there may also be these emerging AI technologies — or whatever. They may even have broken through more technical barriers or gaps in accumulated experience. It's possible — say, a newer generation of creators may then have new technology to support them. Could you define what moment Chinese gaming is in right now? I think Chinese gaming has already moved past the moment of still trying to prove itself — it's becoming more and more ordinary. This identity itself is no longer something that needs to be stressed. But when it reaches that state, I think that's when real development truly begins. Why is that when real development begins? Because your identity no longer matters much. People judge you purely on the game itself. You have to go fight it out in the global market. Then you absolutely must embody that culture or way of thinking in your work. That's not what this is about. You have to be good by any standard. So Chinese gaming seems to have hit a turning point around 2020. Genshin Impact, Black Myth: Wukong, and now Phantom Blade Zero. What do they each represent? I think that was an opening act. Genshin Impact was an opening. I think now we've at least completed the first piece, the first chapter. Including the contribution we've made, we too have proven this may not be a flash in the pan. It exists as a true game product. It should exist as an industry, existing as a product, as an industry. You said last time that Black Myth: Wukong was "the New Culture Movement of the games industry." That's exactly what it is. Because for a game, as a game, and as a cultural product, to truly go global is actually very hard. Only large-scale single-player games can achieve this kind of thorough cultural transmission. But you can't define who's a fish and who's a dragon. So let's talk — will AI have a qualitative impact on the game industry? I think we can approach it from several angles. First: where does a game's perceived value come from? Second, what role can AI actually play in all of this? In truth, much of the time, in many stages of the process, we do have AI assistance. But right now it's mostly in technical areas like logic, like QA, like optimization. In the realm of artistic creation, it's a far more delicate question. Up to now — let me give you the conclusion first: at least in Phantom Blade Zero, we have not used any AI to create the art itself. Not because it creates poorly, and not because we think it has some flaw and not because we think it has some flaw. Because these days, as you can see, there are so many AI web dramas and the like, and the quality they achieve is already quite high. But as far as we're concerned, for now, we do still insist on everything being made by hand. This comes down to the question of where the mass consumer's sense of its value comes from. I think the current situation is something like this: consumers have accepted AI, accepted that AI can create things. But consumers price AI-created work differently. They feel this thing just blew in on the wind — why should you charge me so much for it? Especially in an industry like single-player games, with its very traditional buy-to-own, one-price purchase model. I don't think consumer perception has shifted that far yet. Take two identical things — if one turns out to be AI-made, everyone's value system collapses instantly. For instance, we've all witnessed cases with mobile games where, say, the artwork on gacha cards, the character illustrations, or some promotional images, once discovered to be AI, their perceived value collapsed. Because, as we argued in an article we published: the reason you can sell for that much money isn't that you objectively have this thing, that you objectively produced this thing. I think that only generates half its value. That part can still be perfected. Because this is, after all, something experiential. Call it a work of art or an experiential product of this kind, it is not a purely functional product. A purely functional product — I don't care how it was made, as long as it fulfills its function, it holds 100% of its value. But for single-player games, there's a very subtle question. You first have to work out what exactly the player is buying. The first thing players buy is, of course, the experience. Whoever made it, that experience itself is fine. But what's the further thing players are buying? What players buy — what lies behind that experience? They're buying your labor. They must confirm that you went through years of development, spent hundreds of millions, that this many people built it, pouring in their talent and ingenuity, and that what emerged is genuinely this kind of product. Only then is it worth buying. So why must that labor be part of it? Because it absolutely has to be there. That's the general rule for pricing works of art. Why is it that a Van Gogh hangs in a museum, but the moment you print a copy of it, it's suddenly worthless? Why is the original worth so much? It's the same thing, isn't it? Of course, it doesn't reach the absurd premium of a true masterpiece painting where the original might fetch over a hundred million while the copy is worth nothing at all. That gap is far greater. But here, because there is an artistic element involved, and that artistic element necessarily includes the artists' devotion, the insight the artists brought, and their painstaking effort, the premium that generates rightly belongs in the price. Look, for instance, at Nolan's The Odyssey. Why does he insist on traditional film stock, on projecting traditional film in grand IMAX venues, even though in Asia there may be only one or two such theaters and even North America doesn't have many? Why does he insist on screening it that way? Because it proclaims that he isn't merely providing an experience, he's also telling you what kind of people created that experience. So what you're buying is twofold. You're buying both the product itself and the process of making it that lies behind the product, that painstaking effort. And the same goes for GTA now, GTA 6, right? In the history of games — some say it may be the last blockbuster made the old-fashioned way. But they spent a billion dollars making this magnum opus. Every single element in it was meticulously polished by human hands — and that grants the right to set its price. People say that in the industrial age, in the era of machines, or in the era of photography, this sense of value would be overturned, at least partially overturned. But as you can see, once something involves brand, involves experience, its value is never decided purely by production. Otherwise you'd find that neither cars nor phones would carry any premium. The costs are very low. Once brand premium comes into play, single-player games, for instance, carry a built-in brand premium. As we said earlier — why is it that with single-player games you always know who the development team is? Who made it, who the producer is? Because single-player games inherently carry that brand premium. And that brand premium, to a certain extent, wards off AI — or rather, its logic conflicts with AI's. But even with industrial products — In the industrial age, why do so many luxury brands still insist on showcasing handmade craftsmanship? They want to trumpet that handcrafted making. Though in truth, there's hardly any handcraft left in them now. Those Hermès or LV bags — Most of it is probably made in China — that bag is probably made in China. But at the end, in France, a worker has to attach that final label by hand. That label going on — the moment it goes on, its value rises tenfold. And without it, something produced purely on the manufacturing end, efficiently churned out, identical goods, it's a knockoff worth maybe a tenth of the price. We in China never had that, or rather, only in recent years have we begun to understand where that logic actually lies. But the vast majority of us still judge things by production cost and a production-minded narrative. In other words, we take the very same attitude toward large-scale AI production. It will surely produce in massive quantities. It lowers the barrier for ordinary people to make games, so output grows even larger. But when it comes to brand storytelling, brand narratives are largely immune to this kind of AI disruption, because AI cannot overturn the story behind the brand. Just as industrial production never managed to lower the value of these famous brands, these luxury goods. Yet these brands were mostly founded in an earlier age, in the artisanal, handcraft era. Did industrialized production overturn their value? No. If anything, they still capture the overwhelming share of the value. The deeper we move into the AI era, the more precious old-world craftsmanship becomes. So this argument of yours I think probably does hold true in certain fields today, in fields that carry a brand premium. That is the mightiest premium there is. That kind of artistry and soul is what AI finds hardest to replace. AI will eventually take over most links in production, but the brand built from years of that artistry and soul, that premium — it cannot be replaced. Because by default you trust the feeling that brand evokes. In a sense, would you count as anti-industrialization or anti-AI? I'm not anti-AI — we, I mean, I'm for AI. I'm not anti-AI, I'm pro-AI. Why did so many people pursue the Evil-Warding swordsmanship? Was it really for the Evil-Warding swordsmanship itself?
